"CAPITAL" CHAT.

We have now another distinguished personage in our midst. A man who ia one of the heads of the Mohawk tribe of Canadian Indians, who has been Chief Templar of the world for two years,who is an M.D. of England practising in Toronto, who is Supreme Chief Ranger of the Independent Order of Foresters, and who has come to this country to at- tend the English high courts of that body, is inter- esting. That was decidedly the opinion of the gentlemen, chiefly members of the Order, who have formally welcomed him at the Victoria Hotel, Northumberland Avenue. Dr. Oronhvatekha owes his advancement to the Prince of Wales. Thirty years ago,when His Royal Highness was in Canada, the young Mohawk was deputed to present him with a friendly address. The Prince sent Oron- hyatekba to be educated in England, the result be- ing that he won high honours at Oxford and earned his medical degree. Now one would take him for an English country gentleman,only slightly darker than usual. His son, Dr. Acland Oronbyatekba, shows much more strongly the characteristics of the American aborigine—black eyes and hair, swarthy skin and aquiline nose—but in language, education, and polish he too has got all that West- ern civilisation can give. As for the Independent Order of Foresters, its headquarters are at Toronto, the English branches having been founded only 18 months ago.

Mr. Charles W. Dayton, the postmaster of New York, who is at present visiting London, has been interviewed by the "Sketch." He might be par- doned if he considered himself the busiest man in the world. He superintends the shipment and re- ception of the enormous volume of Transatlantic mails, involving last year sixty-five million letters and postcards. He says the Americans look up to London as a pattern and model in all postal matters. They have nothing like our Parcel Post in the States. Our steady loss in telegraphic business is not an inducement to the American Government to take over their service. Many of the New York letter carriers earn twenty pounds a month. But they have to know all about their districts, to keep track of removals, ferret out obscure addresses, &c., without bothering the head office.

Relics of Old London are fast disappearing. Only a short time ago the overhanging houses in Fleet.st. near Chancery-lane, houses existing at the time of the Plague, gave way to brand-new build- ings. Now the Goose and Gridiron, London House- yard, near St. Paul's Cathedral, and the ancient brick tenement in Fuller's Rents are doomed. In the the former, Freemasonry, as far as this country is concerned,was revived in 1717, and the First Grand Lodge of the World afterwards formed. The latter in Charles H.'s time was "The Squire's," famous as the place where Melbourne and Oates's Club met, and whence many " Spectator " papers are dated. Not long ago it suffered from fire, and was saved only to be sold as old material. Staples Inn, the pride and glory of Holborn, a fine old framework building of great size, with many gables, tiled roof, and each of its four storeys bigger than the next below, still stands. The four or five shops on the ground floor flourish exceedingly, as far as an out- sider can judge,but against it too the edict has gone forth.

An optical and photographic exhibition which will interest all experts and many who are not experts, was opened on Tuesday at the Royal Aquarium. Almost every process is shown, toge- ther with the latest forms of apparatus. There is also much attractiveness in the loan collection. Here may be seen old telescopes and microscopes, and a large sun-dial with a compass for obtaining exact astronomical time. Mr. Dunscombe, of Bris- tol, sends his probably unique collection of spec- tacles, Dutch barnacles, glasses made according to a pattern suggested by Sir Isaac Newton, and" wig spectacles "of the early Georgian period.

The Royal Commission on Vaccination is being urgently pressed to prepare a report, if not its final one, with the least possible delay. It has now been sitting for upwards of five years, and in the mean- time, in consequence of the sanction given to the idea that the matter is sub judice, Boards of Guar- dians throughout the country are sorely perplexed how to carry out their existing duties of enforcing the vaccination laws. Upwards of 120 Boards have, I believe, during the last few weeks passed resolu- tions and addressed communications to the Local Government Board, urging that this long-expected report should be issued, and describing the diffi- culties under which they labour for want of in- structions. On the other hand, I hear that there is little likelihood of the Commission issuing its re- port until after Christmas. The Commission has been overwhelmed with a mass of material, not only from anti-vaccinators, but in the shape of special reports from gentlemen employed for the purpose of collecting evidence regarding all the local epidemics during 1892 and 1893, and the digesting of these documents and the cross-examin- ation of the witnesses has greatly taxed the time at the disposal of the Commissioners for examin- ing all the branches of the subject, which include not only the questiou of the value of vaccination and revaccination as a means of protection against small-pox, but also the question of legal compul- sion.

The pursuit of Thames trout has a singular and almost unintelligible fascination for some anglers. Toe season lasts from the Ist of April to the 10th of September, and a man may fish regularly and at, the end of the season find that he has not taken more than two or three trout. Indeed during the season just over one of the most accomplished and persevering of Thames anglers has not succeeded in bringing a single trout to land. The sport seems to have been unusually bad. In the early days of pisciculture trout fry were turned into the Thames by millions without appreciably increasiwz the number of trout. It is now well understood that a trout has no chance of surviving in a river abound- ing with predatory fish unless it is from one to two years old at the time of its introduction. Quantities of trout large enough to take care of themselves have during the last few years been put in the river, and at Cookham, Maidenhead, Henley, and other places the trout-fishing has thus been greatly improved. But the benefit does sot appear to.have extended itself to the lower waters.

Emboldened by the adoption of the Socialist principle by the Trades Union Congress, the Inde- pendent Labour party have already resolved upen a metropolitan campaign. The members of the con- gress had scarcely reached their homes when both Mr. Tom Mann and Mr. Keir Hardie opened the campaign in different places in London, and it is to be prosecuted with great vigour and energy during the autumn and winter, the object being to estab- lish a branch of the party in every London consti- tuency. For some time meetings of the party have been very frequent, but a complete plan of opera- tions ia now formulated,and the rapidity with which both Mr. Mann and Mr. Hardie pass from the con- gress to their propagandism in the constituencies shows the earnestness and zeal with which the new campaign will be carried on For some time past great. numbers have been joining the party, and the resolution of the congress will, it is expected, lead to a very extensive enrolment of members in nearly all the Radical clubs. Each London constituency in turn will be visited by Mr. Mann and Mr. Hardie, with a view of establishing a branch organization in it ; but at the same time the provinces will not be neglected.

Once more a proposal is made to purchase Carlyle's house in Cheyne-row, and it may be that, like previous proposals, it will prove abortive. Cheyne-row is one of the streets of Chelsea, and abuts at right angles upon the river—a street with no pretensions, and containing very commonplace semi-detached brick houses, one of which was Carlyle's heme for 47 years. The house is now fall- ing into decay—why, it has not been stated—and this is all the more remarkable, as the proprietor believes he holds a historic treasure. At least, he will not sell the dingy old building except at an exorbitant price, which nobody is disposed to give. There are two odd things about the announce- ment of a coming party at Balmoral to celebrate the birth of an heir to the throne. One, in view of the considerable time that has elapsed since the event, is the belated character of the celebration. The other is that, amid all the distinguished names given,there is no mention of the Duke and Duchess of York, or of the infant Prince Edward of York. The Queen's sons and daughters, sons-in-law and daughters-in-law, grandsons and granddaugters, are there in profusion, but the infant Hamlet and his parents seem to have no part in the play.

Do fruiterers make prodigious profits? If we look only at the wholebale prices in Covent Garden Market and the retail figures elsewhere we shall be tempted to answer" Yes." A bushel of fine pears— Williams, tor example—can be got for half-a-crown. It contains an average of 175 pears, which the shop- kPeper usually vends at a penny each, reaping four- teen shillings and sevenpence where he has sown half-a-crown. If he sells two for three-halfpence the revenue is ten shillings and tenpence. Lest, however, these figures should cause a rush to the fruit trade, it should be borne in mind that the wholesale dealers not only take no responsibility for damaged fruit, but permit no examination of the " lots " beyond a view of the tops. The tact is not generally known. It appears to be itznered by some persons who are just now complaining. Then the retail dealer must sell his pears quickly or they decay on his hands. To those, therefore, who do a large business the profits are big, but not to others.

The Mission of the Rev. John M`Neill, the "Scottish Spurgeon," in Australia has, I learn, been a great success. His preaching in Melbourne has attracted great crowds of all classes. It was begun in the Congregational chapel, but that quickly proved too small, and the Town Hall was engaged. This in turn became too small, and the great Exhi- bition building was secured. The experiment was regarded with doubt by a few, but was quickly justified by the result. On the first Sunday after- naon the building was packed an hou r before the time of service and at night it was necessary to shut and lock the doors as soon as the building was filled in order to prevent a rush. The meetings are said to be the largest religious gatherings ever held in the colony.

The Portuguese say that no man can be a good hus- band who does not gat a good breakfast. In France one hundred and forty-eight thousand eight hundred and eight families have claimed exemption from certain taxes recently voted by Government,on account of having seven or more children.

In Sweden, if you address the poorest person in the street you must lift your hat. The same courtesy is insisted upon if you pass a lady on the stairway. To enter a reading-room or a bank with one's hat on is re- garded as impolite. Of sixty-seven queens of France, only thirteen have died without leaving their histories a record of misery. Eleven were divorced, two executed, nine died young, seven were widowed, three cruelly treated, three exiled the poisoned and broken-hearted mak e up the rest.

Megrim.

Let us now consider what can be done in the way of treatment for megrim. There appears to be a very prevalent opinion that megrim is a complaint in whicn it is no use trying to do anything—an opinion with which we venture to disagree, for we must confess that we have an almost unlimited faith in the power of medicines ; that is, of medicines properly used. Of course somethieg can be done in the way of general treatment—hygienic measures, and so on. The patient may have to be instructed what to eat and what te drink, and still more important, what to avoid. Megrim is says "The Family Physician," of constant occurrence in those who are weakened by a Nor and insufficient diet, by too frequent child-bearing, and a prolonged suckling. It often arises from excessive hours of labour, or occupations which entail close confinement in un- wholesome and ill-ventilated workshops and dwellings. The treatment of these cases is obvious,however difficult to fulfil. The workman may not tie able to induce his employer to get him a light, well-ventilated shop to work in, but knowing the value of fresh air, he will pass as much of his leisure time as possible out of doors. Women often ruin their health by suckling their children for twelve, fifteen, or eighteen months. With town-dwellers the baby should be weaned at the latest when nine months old. The poor should remember that if they have large families they must make an extra effort to provide for them. In a somewhat higher grade of society we find the malady brought on, or at all events aggravated, by excessive brain-work, with a deficiency of bodily exercise, short restless nights, and insufficient sleep. Se long as a brain-Worker is able to sleep well, to eat well,and to take a fair proportion of outdoor exercise, it is not necessary to impose any special limits on the actual number of hours he devotes to his labours. But when what is generally known as worry steps in to com- plicate matter, when cares connected with family arrangements, or with those numorous personal details which we can seldom escape intervene, or when the daily occupation of life is in itself a fertile source of anxiety, then we find one or other of these three safe- guards broken down. Probably the man of business or the successful lawyer fai!s to shake himself free from his anxieties at night, and slumber becomes fitful or dis- turbed. The nervous system, unsettled by the mental strain brings about various defects in nutrition; the appetite fails ; and then we meet the sleeplessness the dyspepsia, the irresolution, the irritability, and the depression which are the chief miseries of the over- worked. The great thing in these cases is to get rest at any cost. By rest we do not mean doing nothing, but rather change of scene, of thought, and occupation. If you tell a busy man that he must do nothing, he may endeavour to obey you, but he will soon find out that he cannot, for his brain keeps on working in the same old groove, and he is as much, or even more, worried about his business as if he were still in the thick of it. The great thing is to get a rest by substituting one kind of work for an other, to have for a time a nice comfortable sort of occupation to replace the old weary round of troubles. One of the most important remedial agents is outdoor life and exercise, which may be taken in any form most congenial to the individual—riding, walking, field sports, or what not. This is at once the most natural, and often the most effectual promoter of sleep we can employ. Active bodily exertion is well known to be incompatible with the maximum of intellectual work, and full advantage should be taken of this fact. The only thing to avoid is excessive fatigue. It is a remarkable fact that a very large numbtr of distinguished literary and scientific men have suffered severely from megrim, and it would seem that some of them have succeeded in ridding themselves of the malady by the adoption of some simple hygienic measure. One, for instance, cured himself by following the prescription of a farrier, who advised him to drink water, eat little, and take exercise. Another was cured by drinking every day a I arge quantity of fresh water. and .exchanging a highly nutritious regimen for a much lighter dietary. A third got rid of his old enemy by the same means, and by taking exercise every day before dinner. There can be no doubt that in many cases great benefit would be derived from a thorough change of locality or climate. Long sea-voyages are not infrequently attended with excellent results, the attacks being absent for months at a time. Unfortunately, these are remedies not within the reach of all.

DESPERATE COIN PLOT WITH A BURGLAR.

At the North London Police Court on Tuesday Edward Purdoe, aged 24, was remanded on the charge of bur- glary. Police Constable Piper discovered the prisfener early on Tuesday morning in the breakfast-room of a house in Grosvenor-road,London. The man endeavoured to make the officer believe that he was really in his own house, and that" his mother was upstairs ; " but the police received his story with incredulity, and after a humorous clnversation the two men had a fierce struggle. The constable was wounded, one of his hands being severly cut with a knife ; but he finally succeeded, with assistance, in arresting the prisoner. The burglar was not overpowered, however, till he had flung the officer down the area steps.

AN UNDISCOVERED DISCOVERER.

All the great scientific men were for the time beaten. Something was wanted to prevent photographs which were distinctly visible in the dark from turning black when exposed to light. Sir Humphry Davy,the inventor of the miners' safety-lamp, failed to get over the diffi- culty; while Daguerre. the French artist, who had already done a greal deal for photography, was still experimenting without success. At an optician's in Paris, one day, a poorly-dressed and famished-looking youth inquired the price of a camera that was to be seen in the window. Tne sun: named was, says " Little Folks," far too high for the pocket of the lad, and as his face showed the disappointment he felt, the shopkeoper, out of curiosity, asked why the instrument was wanted. The youth replied that he had succeeded in solving the difficulty already mentioned, but that he wished to get a new and better camera in order to continue his experi- ments. At this the optician laughed, and asked the young inventor if he knew that all the attempts made by many well-known scientific men had failed. In answer to this tho lad pulled out from his pocket a piece of paper on which was a view of Paris, at the same time remark- ing, "This is what I can obtain." The astonished shop- keeper proceeded to question the stranger on the discov- ery, and at last obtained from him a little bottle containing some liquid which would bring about the desired result. The youth then left, promising to call on the following day. As soon as he had gone the optician attempted to follow the instructions given to him with the phial, but without any success whatever. The re-appearance of the stranger was looked forward to anxiously, but nothing was ever again seen or heard of him. Daguerre in time accomplished the task; but there can be little doubt that the unknown and ill-clad youth was really the first to discover the secret of making a photograph permanent.

FACE TO FACE WITH A BOA.

The Boa Constrictor has a long scaly head, which is broad behind, and the tail has a single row (.4 subcaudal scales. They are arboreal, and watch for their prey. swooping down on it head first, seizing and coiling their long and stout body around it. They teach twelve feet in length as a rule, and it is said that some are twice and more as long, but there are grave doubts about the truth of the statement. The ornamentation is rather peculiars and there is a long series of markings extend- ing the whole length, composed alternately )1 great blackish stains or spots irregularly hexagonal, and of pale oval stains or spots notched or jagged at either end the whole forming a very elegant pattern. It has the habits of the family,and is restricted to the tropical parts of South America. Probably this was the Snake which was worshipped by the natives, and it bas a strange literature attached to it of stories of the most wonderful kind, and it has been confounded with the Anaconda, which forms the next genus of the sub-family. Bates once, on an insect-hunting expedition, met a Boa Con- strictor face to face, The huge serpent was coming down a slope, and making the dry twigs crack and fly with its weight, as it moved over them. He knew there was no danger and stood his ground, and the reptile suddenly turned and glided at an accellerated pace down the path. The rapidly-moving a rid shining body looked like a stream of brown liquid flowing over the thick bed of fallen leaves rather than a Serpent with a skin of varied colours. One morning after a night of deluging rain at Para, the lamplighter,on his rounds to oat inguish the lamps, knocked Bates up to show him a Boa Con- strictor he had just killed in the street not far off. He had cut it nearly in two with his knife as it was making its way down the sandy street.

LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE, Some smart crepon gowns in black and colours which I have just seen made me feel how necessary at least one dress of it is to complete a wardrobe. One of soft lavender, made over buttercup-yellow silk, has the skirt edged with a frill of black and satin ribbon, two lengthy ends of the black falling over the back of the gown. The bodice is quite unique, with three coachman's capes of black satin, lined with yellow, coming just below the some- what full yoke, bands of black ribbon coming over the front of the bodice and meeting the far,,. - ends at the back of the waist.

Felt curl beaver hats have already put in an appt a-anze, although August is barely out, and for travelling and country wear they are almost more popular than straws. The styles and shapes which are most in favour at the moment are rather wide brimmed boat or Amazon shapes in two or three varieties, and the well-known Toreador or Bolero hat, with its all-round upturned brim and cluster of rosettes or pompons on the left side, which was so fashionable a couple of years ago. The first style of hat in decidedly the most becoming to ordinary folk, and in soft cedar or tan felt, with a side trimming of three or four upstanding loops of brown ribbon, and a couple of white or black wings, it is as sensible and yet as pretty a head- covering as one could desire. Birds and wings are indeed to be seen on nine felt hats out of 10, and the tenth is generally adorned with cock's plumes or towering ostrich tips. Gauze is a favourite trimming for the early autumn felts, and mixed with wings it looks exceedingly well, although it is, of coarse, not so serviceable as ribbon or velvet.

A bean tifu ea gown which I saw recently is made of turquoise-blue satin merveilleux, with a complete overdress, falling from the shoulders, of crocodile ortipon of the same shade, striped with fine Irish lace insertion. The front of the dress was composed of flounces of accordion-pleated ohlff)n, also blue, and the sleeves, which boasted epaulettes of lace, were made of satin, with a deep ruche of chiffon at the elbow. A successful rival to this was a gown of mauve and white striped silk crepon, with a wonder- ful lace collar over the shoulders, display- ing a front of accordion-pleated white crêpe de Chine, caught on either side with a rosette of mauve watered ribbon, falling with ends to the hem. At the neck of this was a little ruche of mauve chiffon. The effect was charming, and charming, too, was a gown made of black and white striped brocade, with the front of white chiffon, and braces of sequined net, lined with white, :taught at the ends with black satin bows : the long basques which fell below the knees were made of white Limerick lace, the same also trim- ming the elbow sleeves, while at the neck the gown was slightly décolleté, soft frills of chiffon outlin:ng it most becomingly. As to garden party toilette I noticed a stylish oostume with the blouse and wrist flounce in earn embroidery. Short draped sleeves and skirt in ivory barege were mot off with small rosette bows in satin ribbon. The hat was in fancy straw, ornamented with lace, an aigrette, and variegated garden poppies.

It will be with a keen interest, says CameWs Family Magazine for September, we shall grasp the novel details of next month's designs. At present there remain to us many that may be recommended as possessing a charm for some time to come: in these it is noteworthy that the ten- dency of broad-brimmed hats is to turn upwards around the edge, resting with becoming effeet on a bandeau or coronet of velvet and small rosettes. The crowns, if small, are assertive in form, one peculiar exception to the round-shaped crown being arranged to form four peaked sections. As an • example of what is now worn in large hats, I will quote two strikingly handsome specimens in pale lemon-coloured straw trirnmel with black velvet and tips—one with the v. ito brim caught up in front by two bands of black ribbon velvet to a centre group of three lovely white ostrich tips; the other of shallower brim encrusted with tiny jet sequins, raised all ronnd and caught in at intervals by blank velvet rosettes, black tips gracefully placed at one aide and a knot with ends of pink miroir velvet set in front of the crown. Yours lovingly, jENNLII.

LADY CYCLISTS AND THE "RATIONAL"

COSTIIM L.

A LONG RIDE.

Miss Bacon, the indefatigable, and perhaps the most practical, advocate on rational dress in London, has just returned from a successful tour of 1,200 miles on her bicycle. She started on the 3rd August, in the early dawn, for Suffolk, wandering round the East Coast for several days, and thence via Peterborough, York, and Durham, to Edinburgh, where she met with an accident which necessitated an immediate journey to Glasgow for repairs. This caused so much delay that she had to abandon her original intention of csntinuing north to the Trossachs, Oban,and Inverness, and she accordingly started on her homeward journey on 17th August,taking the Falls ot Clyde on her way to Carlisle. She spent four days among the lakes, where she met a National Home Reading Party.who were duly impressed with her costume and deplored their own petticoated conditicn, especially when, alter ascending Skiddaw, they found that their muddy and bedraggled appearance did not compare well with Miss Bacon's neat and still unsoiled costume. From Kendal she dived into the depths of the northern Back Country, riding for twenty miles to Warrington. She was glad to leave Lancashire behind and to find herself in beautiful historic Chester, whence she cycled into Wales to visit Conway, Bangor, and Car- narvon. where she met the first and only sign of aston- ishment on the part of the natives. She suffered no discomfort, however, and received better treatment than did a lady and gentleman on a tricycle whom she had met a few days previously en route, who had met with the rudest behaviour, and had been followed in some places for distances by interested crowds. The lady wore a skirt, and this Miss Bacon holds as the reason for the incivility. She herself rode the whole way in her rational dress,.and visited the cathedrals in her knicker- bockers without attracting attention or creating any remark.

By the Author of " DARICWOOD."

CHAPTER XXXII. HOPES AND FEARS.

The joys of meeting pay the pangs of absence; Else who would bear it? _ ROWE.

Lo-rd Grosvenor sprang up and opened the door, his face beaming a welcome. The marquis, leaning on his staff, was standing in the wide hall without, issuing commands to a small audience of servants. At sight of the young owner of the Eyrie his countenance changed with marvellous rapidity, and he came forward bowing with old-time courtesy. "I have received a message that my niece is here, Lord Grosvenor," he exclaimed, removing his hat. "Be kind enough to conduct me to her at once."

"Miss Arevalo is here, my lord," said Giralda's lover, courteously, opening the parlour door more widely. •" Come in." The marquis bowed again and approached the oriel parlour. Giralda arose in agitation as she heard him coming, and stood facing the door and trembling like a culprit. She was in some awe of her stern old kinsman, and a fear crossed her mind now that he would never forgive her in- tended flight and receive her back to his home and his love.

His lordship came in slowly and stood against the door as his young host closed it, leaning on his staff and looking at the young girl with a stern gravity that almost broke her heart.

"What does this mean, Giralda ?" he said, not angrily, but very sternly, as he looked from her white face to the gay little breakfast-table with its festive air. "I went up to your room last night, before my nephew went away, and discovered your note. I set out immediately in search of you, half wild with anxiety. I looked for you along the road, at the station, through the streets of Trevelyan village. And then I went back over the road and searched for hours, fancying you might have fallen, In the darkness, from some bridge, or into some chasm. I have had my servants out all night. I went home two hours since, wholly wild and des- pairing, and received Lord Grosvenor's message that you were here. I set out for Eyrie at once—to find you enjoying yOurself, and very much at your ease, as it seems r he added, bitterly. Giralda looked up at him indigantly, but the sight of his worn and haggard face melted her heart. His anxieties in her behalf had changed him greatly. He looked like one who has been through the anguish of a peat affliction. "Uncle 1" she said, softly, approaching him. "Dear uncle I Must I defend myself against you ?" The marquis started, and the sternness fell from his face like a mask. He could not resist the ten- der pleading of that innocent, child-like counten- ance. He opened his arms, and Giralda sprang into them, sobbing, and weeping. Lord Grosvenor saw the old lord's tears dropping like rain among those dusky, clustering curls. "Come and sit down here, and tell me all about It," said the marquis, huskily, as soon as he could command his voice, drawing Giralda to a sofa. "Now, why did you run away, my child? And why, if there was need of your immediate presence at your home, did you come to the Eyrie ? ' "Let me explain it to you, my lord," said Giralda's lover's, eagerly. "I have heard the whole story from Miss Arevalo, and she is tired." The marquis bowed assent. Lord Grosvenor gave Giralda a reassuring look, and said:

"Miss Arevalo had been out riding, and had met with an accident and adventure, of which we will say more hereafter. That was yesterday morning. On entering the park grounds, and learning that Lord Adlowe had arrived, she decided to walk up to the house and enter unobserved by a side porch. She passed throagh the garden, and suddenly stopped, hearing voices in the summer-house. They were the voices of Lord Adlowe and his valet."

"Well, my lord?" said the marquis, knitting his bushy white brows together. ",It happened that Lord Adlowe saw Miss Are- valo. He came out of the summer-house and drew her within it, sending his man away. He told her he loved her and asked her to be his wife. Miss Arevalo rejected him." "He told me this last night," said the marquis, his face clouding. "What Was there in his offer to cause my adopted niece to flee from under my roof ? "

"Lord Adlowe did not accept his rejection. He told her that he would wreak vengeance on her parents. He said she should sutler through her father and her mother. The poor "child whom he threatened in such a dastardly manner resolved to make known their fancied peril, and the threats of Adlowe to her parents at once." "My poor child," said Lord Trevelyan, gather- ingthe young girl again tenderly to his bosom. "Why didn't you come to me with your story! The dastardly wretch I To threaten my ward whom I have warned him to let alone."

"Miss Arevalo did not go to you, my lord," said Lord Grosvenor, "because she believed she could not do so without making a serious trouble, and that she shrank from. She resolved to steal away alone and on foot to the station. She did not feel strong enough to face you and to keep her secret. She bore her anxieties and griefs in the seclusion of her chamber until evening, and then set out alone for Trevelyan "But she was not there, nor on the road, when I sought her 1" exclaimed the marquis, puzzled. "And she is here? I cannot understand—"

"I Will make all plain to you very soon, my lord," said Giralds.'s young lover, noting her trembling figure, and desirous of concluding a scene so painful to her. "As I said, Miss Arevalo net out for the station. She had not travelled half the distance when she was overtaken by a light waggon, in which two men were sitting. Those Then were Lord Adlowe and his valet. They seized Miss Arevalo, despite her prayers and struggles, bound her, after pulling her into the waggon, and returned on their course, Lord Adlowe alighting at the Park—"

"This is monstrous ! montrous ;" interrupted the marquis, rousing himself from the sort of stupor into which he hail momentarily falln. " aaa scarcely credit it! It sounds like a leaf out 02 some romance I must be dreaming ! " "It is true!" declared Lord Grosvenor. "Lord Adlowe's man carried Miss Arevalo on to the bluff cottage, putting her in charge of Mrs. Bittly, who had been previously warned, and who had prepared a secure prison for the young lady!" "Mrs. Bittly ! She was Adlowe's nurse, and has always thought much of him 1" muttered the marquis. Lord Grosvenor told the remainder of the story briefly—how he had chanced to be sailing, how Giralda had attracted his attention, and how he had rescued and brought her to the Eyrie.

"She wanted to return to you last night, my lord," he concluded, "but she marl too near ill. I sent the messenger to assure you of her safety ! " Lord Trevelyan had listened to the narration with frequent '.nterruptions, his face expressing all the stormy emotions of which his strong nature was capable. Rage, astonishment, incredulity, and sheer anger, by turns convulsed his features. Had Adlowe been present, he might almost have been transfixed with one of the lurid lightning flashes that leaped from the marquis's eyes. "From this moment my d00..a shall be closed to Lord Adlowe ! " he said, in a low, concentrated voice, his face gathering blackness. "From this moment I wash my hands of him—the wretch, the coward ! Strange, is it not?" he added, with sav- age bitterness. "The Trevelyans were once a race esteemed, and noted even for their honour, their true nobleness, their high integrity! You know now how the race has died out, Lord Grosvenor-- what pitiful specimens the two latest scions of my house have shown themselves ! The one ungrate- ful, a schemer, a robber, an intended assassin— may his bones never rest in peace l The other an insulter of women, a terrifier of innocence and helplessness—a schemer too ! Wretches I foul wretches both ! "

Giro.lda clung to him, frightened at his vehe- mence.

lie arose and put her from him with infinite gentleness, standing before her, haggard and distressful, yet with a tender, loving light on his face.

"My child," he said—and his voice was sad now, intensely sad—" when I urt cd you to become the light of my home, the pet of my old age, I promised you and your mother to shield you from all dangers and all sorrows with my lite. How have I fulfilled my trust ? I promised to guard you from that villain. How have I kept that promise? I have admitted him to my house' been blind to his persecutions of you, permitted him to insult and threaten you, and finally to immure you in a. prison I have been unfaithful to the charge re- posed in me. I deserve my fate—to lose you. You will go away from me to your former home," and his voica faiteked. " I 111 be justly left to my desolation, now doubly 4reary that I have

The road was passed over quickly, the young lord having little time to spare. Lord Grosvenor sprang out, assisting the mar- quis to the porch. Then he gave his hand to Gir- alda, detaining her long enough to whisper: "I will write at once, as soon as I shall have seen them, unless I return immediately. Trust ia me, Giralda. Believe that all will be well." "If you save them from Adlowe's hatred aq revenge, you will save my life yet the third time, responded Giralda, agitatedly. "I do trust you, Paul. I shall pray for you. I shall be eager to hear from and see you again." FLOWER SHOW AT CROFT.

The annual flower show at Croft was held on Saturday. There were a good number of entries. The judges were Messrs. Harrison and Jackson, of Newton, and the following composed the com- mittee :-Messrs. J. Shaw (chairman), W. Fazaker- ley, A. Monks, A, Marsh, T. Abbey, N. Taylor, jun., A. 'Willington, and J. Green; hon. treasurer, Mr. joen Samson ; and hon. secretary, Mr. J. Cawthorne. The garden inspectors were Messrs. Fazakerley and Alfred Dixon. The show was opened at four o'clock, when there were present the Rev. H. I. Wadeson (rector ' of Croft, the president of the society) and Mrs. Wadeson, the Rev. C. Bowden (Flaydock), Captain and Mrs. With- ington, Miss and Miss M. Comber, Mr. Hugh Gooch (Newton), Mr.R.Owee.sen.,and Mr.R.Owen.jun. (Croft), Mr. and Mrs. Bellamy (Croft), ,k 3. The Rector, president of the society,opened the exhibition with a few remarks Mr. Harrison, one of the judges, also spoke.-Mr. Caw- thorne (secretary) proposed, and Captain Withington seconded, a vote of thanks to the president. This was accorded, and the visitors then proceeded to inspect the exhibits. The following is the prize list :- Class I. Plants.-Three greenhouse plants, distinct: 1, W. Fazakerley ; 2, H. Green. A. BABY IN A. BUTTER TUB.

At St. George's-in-the-East Vestry Hall, London, on Tuesday, Mr. Wynne E. Baxter, the East London coroner,resumed the inquiry r9specting the death of the illegitimate male child of Betsy Webb, a young laundry woman who stands remanded from the Thames Police Court on the charge of concealing the birth of her child. The evidence adduced at the previous hearing disclosed a shocking story. In May last, Betsy Webb, whose age was given as twenty-two, was confined of a child at 9, Fordham-street, Mile-end. Her sister, who was under seventeen years of age, and was herself a mother,resided with her, and at times they had been so destitute that they were absolutely without a particle of food either for themselves or their little ones, and were without fir- ing or light. About four weeks ago they removed to 68 Anthony-street, St. George's, where thay occupied a back bedroom on the first floor. The landlady,for some reason unexplained, became suspicious of the contents of a butter tub which the girls brought with them, and on lookinginside shewas horrifiedtofind the mummified body of a child. The medical evidence showel that a piece of

linen, twisted so as to form a chord, was tied y -tightl round the child's neck, and that its skull was also frac- tured.—The jury, after a short private consultation, re- turned a verdict of " Wilful murder" against Betsy Webb.

IHE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 15' 1894.

THE SHIP CANAL

MISHAP TO A STEAMER NEAR WARRINGTON.

About 1.50 on Tuesday morning the large three-masted steamer " Telegraphic " from Belfast, ran aground near to the proposed entrance to Walton Lock from the Canal. It appears that at the time there was a thick fog, and the lights on the dredger working at the pro- posed entrance to the lock, misled those in charge of the steamer, and the vessel ran into the bank which divides the canal and the old river bend at Wilderspool. The prow of the vessel became imbedded in the loose sand of the bank and she remained in this position until ten o'clock the same morning when with the assistance of the tide and the steam tug " Agnes Seed" she was towed off and proceeded on her voyage to Manchester apparently not much worse for the accident. Some con- siderable damage was done to the bank. OPENING OF A PONTOON DRY DOCK. The formal opening of a floating pontoon dry dock, belonging to the Manchester Ship Canal Pontoons and Dry Docks Company, and situate at Mode Wheel, Man- chester, took place on Saturday afternoon, There was a large company at the ceremony. Mr. George Renwick, the chairman of the Pontoon Company, and Messrs. Alexander Taylor, Joseph Eltringham and Thomas Bell, directors, were present. The Ship Canal Company were represented by Sir Bosdin T. Leech, one of the Corpora- tion directors; Sir Leader Williams, the engineer; and Mr. Marshall Stevens, the manager. The machinery was set in motion by Mrs. A. Taylor, to whom was pre- sented a handsome bouquet. The invited guests were afterwards entertained by the company, Mr. Renwick presiding. Captain Croft, in proposing success to the Pontoons and Dry Nicks Company, predicted that the canal would prove a great success.—The Chairman, in response,said he, too was of the same opinion, and the fact that the Newcastle people had put their money into the Pontoon Company showed that they also were sanguine as to the success of the canal. The Horaiten, 300 ft. long and 40ft. beam, had safely come up the canal drawing 22ft., and was now discharging 800 standards of timber, and this was proof that the largest trading vessels could safely navigate the waterway. It only remained now for Man- chester trades to take advantage of the facilities placed at their disposal to make the canal a great success. Be thought the directors should turn their attention to bulky cargo more than to Manchester goods, and then ship- owners would be only too anxious to send their vessels to Manchester.—Mr. Thomas Bell (Newcastle), in pro- posing "Success to the Canal," said the more he saw of. the waterway, the more confidence he had in its ultimate success.—Sir Bosdin Leech, who replied, said people had expected too much of the canal in its first year. His opinion was that they had done very fairly considering everything. He thought that when present arrangements with the railway companies for example, were properly completed, the traffic would immensely increase, and before many years were over the ordinary shareholders would be receiving a dividend. There was a determina- tion on the part of Manchester men tbat they would not be beaten in this matter, and the canal was therefore bound to succeed.—The health of Sir Leader Williams and Mr. Marshall Stevens was proposed, and the latter gentleman, in the course of his reply, said that during the time the canal had been open for traffic they had done as well as they could have expected. It was only a matter of time before they would have all the traffic they wanted. ( Applause.)

===== 0002601_18940915_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COTTON TRADE AND THE CANAL.

At a meeting of the North Moor Spinning Company held on Wednesday night Mr. Daniel Marsland, chair- man, called upon the people of Oldham to support the Ship Canal as much as possible by bringing all cotton direct to Manchester. A day was lost by doing so, but that was not much, and they could buy cotton a day earlier. Oldham ought to set the example in this matter, and show to people in other parts that they were deter- mined to support the canal. If Oldham would support it in the manner which he desired the directots of the Canal Company would no doubt endeavour to get cotton shipped in as little time as they got it now. The canal was worthy of being suppprted by Lancashire commer- cial men, and he thought Oldham would be well re- paid.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
TECHNICAL EDUCATIONAT ST. HELENS.

The second annual report of Mr. Jeff. J. Broom- head, director of technical education for the county borough of St. Helens, just issued, deals with (1) elementary education, (2) science and techical edu- cation, (3) manual instruction,and (4) generally. Under the first head Mr. Broomhead states that there were six evening continuation schools, with a number of 297 on the register and an average attendance of 134,against five schools in the previous session. with 490 on the re- gister and an average attendance of 146. The work done had undoubtedly been good, but was still maimlv of a very elementary nature, though it was to be noted that such subjects as French.shorthand,commersial arithme- tic,and book-keeping had been taught. It was,of course, impossible, to make the work of the schools more ad- vanced until students could be found prepared to take the advanced work, and this could only be done by in- creasing the school life of the scholars in the day schools. As regarded science and technical instruction during the past session, the Cowley Schools and the College of Pupil Teachers had held day science classes. At the evening science and art classes seventeen subjects had been taken, the number of entries being 534, with an average attendance of 342, and the number of papers worked 312, against fifteen subjects, 472 entries, 300 average attendance, and 287 papers worked in the pre- vious session. In the cookery classea there had been 392 pupils and 320 lessons, against 340 pupils and 306 lessons in the previous session. The average attendance was eighteen per lesson, as against twenty-four the pre- vious year. In manual instruction there had been thirty-two entries,with an average attendance of twenty- four and twenty-two examined, against thirty-four en- tries, twenty-nine average attendance and thirty-two examined in the previous session. The number of scho- lars from elementary schools taking woodwork had in- creased from 162 to 269. Speaking generally, the num- ber of passes secured this session was 195, against 108, an increase of 87,and the number of papers worked 380, against 376. The percentages had increased by forty- six in art and about sixteen in science and techno- logy.

There are one thousand seven hundred and eighty- five separate railway companies in the United States.

WARORAVE CHURCH. EARLESTOWN.

The annual special services of thanksgiving for the ingathering of the harvest were commenced on Thursday, last week, and continued on Sunday. In the above church. The interior bore abundant evi- dence of the season, the chancel and font being prettily decorated with flowers, fruit, and vegetables, there being a plentiful minding of heather. The win- dow recesses were also well laden with flowers and fruit, and altogether the church bore a very pleasing appearance. The work of decorating had been carried out by Mesdames Carson, Collingwood, Norman,Thomp- son, Pryor Misses Carson, Thompson, Laithwaite,Kean, Mayne, eorlett, Wilson, Banks, and Mr. J. Carson. There was a moderate congregation on Thursday even- ing,when an earnest sermon appropriate to the occasion was preached by the Rev. John Davies, M.A., vicar of Newsome, Yorkshire. During the service the choir sang the anthem "Ye shall dwell in the Land," Miss F. L. Kean and Mr. Pryor contributing solos. On Sunday morning the service was conducted by the Rev. J. Carson, rector, and in the afternoon a musical service was given by the choir and organist (Mr. E. Leyland). The programme which was a very chaste one, and included some very difficult airs from the oratorios. The choruses were remarkably well rendered. The opening piece was the solo and chorus "As pants the hart," Miss F. L. Kean taking the lead- ing part, in which she acquitted herself with excellent taste. The chorus was very well sung, the blending of the Voices being beautifully brought out and maintained. The baritone solo " Thou're passing hence," was sung by Mr. C. H. Lea in a manner which left nothing to be desired, but Miss A. Molyneux in "Return, 0 God of Hosts," could hardly be considered to be up to her usual standard, yet she gave a very careful interpretation. The anthem "Hearken unto me," was capitally sung, the different parts being briskly taken up. The orgaa solo " Cverture (Holy City ") was given by Mr. Leyland in his usual clever style, his manipulation of the organ being exceptionally fine. The air "Lord God of Abra- ham." by Mr. Pryor was hardly given with the desired effect, but the quartet "Cast thy burden," from the same erotorio, was very nicely sung. Miss F. L. Kean gave a pleasing interpretation of the air "How beauti- ful are the feet," from the Messiah. The air "Sound an alarm." was given with nr,uch expression and in good voice by Mr.J Nichols,and the chorus "We hear" (Judas Maccabeus) was efficiently rendered. The organ solo "Harvest Festival March," needs no comment, and the same may be said of the " Hallelujah Chorus,"whichwas given with much force. At the evening service there was a crowded congrega- tion, and the pulpit was occupied by the Rev. R. G. Matthew, vicar of St. Michael's, Wigan, who in the course of his discourse spoke of the relation of seed to education, and illustrated the sending of the Apostles into all the world to preach the Gospel, contending that God meant to win the world by the ministry of men. In many of the schools they were putting the bible int* the hands of the scholars who read it without comment. That was not the way the bible was intended to be read. It was to be used by men who knew what it was. The bible was not the book to be thrown among people for them to gather their own faith from. If they were to sow seed in their children's hearts, which was to bring forth life everlasting, it must be good seed, and that seed was the word of God. They nut the bible into the schools' and teld the teachers to teach the bible from a purely historical points, and without any practical or dogmatical comments. It was quite possible to make the bible an obstacle instead of a help to the faith. It was a very disappointing work, teaching the children in schools, they taught them prayer, and service of the church, and then they saw them growing up to young men, and young women, getting careless, and going away from church, and all the seed was wasted, but there would be seed tbat would fall on the way side, and and among thorns, which would spring up and bear fruit of holy things. The collections, during the whole of the services, in aid of the school building improvement fund, amounted to £l5 Bs. sd.

WILFUL MURDER AT GRAPPENHALL

On Monday afternoon,as several persons were go- ing along the bank of the river Mersey near Pad- dington Lock, they noticed a parcel inside a ham- per. Upon pulling it out it was discovered to be that of a young child with a weight attached to it. Information was at once given to the police. and the body was conveyed to the Dog and Dart Inn to await an inquest. THE INQUES r.

The inquest was held at the Dog and Dart, Grappen- hall, on Wednesday, by Dr. BI unt, deputy-coroner for Mid-Cheshire. Thomas Baldry, 16, Stephen-st., Wairington, deposed that on Monday afternoon, about two o'clock, he was walking along the Mersey, close to Paddington Lock, when his attention was called by a man named Woods to something in the river. He went round and found it to be the body of a child. With assistance he got the body out of the water, and reported the mater to the police at Padgate. The body was about seven yards from the bank, and was in a large hamper about five feet long. There was no clothing whatever upon the body, but there was a wire wound round it, and at the end of the wire was attached a weight of about 11b. The body must have been in the river a fortnight. It ap- peared to be that mf a newly-born child.—By the fore- man (Mr. Leyland) : The child was not fastened in the hamper, to which there was no lid. The wire was twisted as well as wound round the child: - .

_ P.C. Millington said that about 4.30 on Monday he received information of the discovery, and went to Paddington Locks. He there saw the body which was that of a male child, much decomposed. He removed the body to the Dog and Dart. He thought it was a newly-born child. The hamper had been in the river for over three months, and it was owing to the river being lower than usual that the body was seen. The police had made inquiries about the neighbourhood, but they had no reason to suspect anyone. The body was pulled out of the river on the Cheshire side. The Coroner in summing up said this was one of those unsatisfactory cases which occurred from time to time. It appeared to him that the fact of the wire having been placed around the body in two or three places and at- tached to a weight, pointed out that it had been inten- tionally put into the water, and it seemed to him that the proper verdict to be giver: was that of " Wilful murder against some person or persons unknown." The jury after about a quarter of an hour's delibera- tion, returned the verdict suggested by the Coroner.

CAN LIFE BE PURCHASED?

It would sometimes seem as though it could. A rich man who is weary or sick can take rest and obtain the best medical aid, for lack of which a poor man may die. But in those noble institutions the Hospitals, the highest medical skill and the best medicines are bestowed freely, even on the poor ; and thus, though the rich seem to buy long life, the poor are not debarred from it, so fast as medicine can help. There is help, too, sometimes, even when medicine fails, as an interesting report in the Bournemouth papers shows. Mr. T. Bison, of e, Tense Cottages, West Hill-road, Bournemouth, suffered a year all the miseries of acute bronchitis, which incapacitated I him from work, notwithstanding the patient efforts of a most skilful and attentive doctor. Last September he was received into a Convalescent Home, but in Decem ber had to enter the Hospital at Bournemouth, where all that science could do, apparently, was done for him. Medicine, however, was of no avail, until, learning .of the extraordinary advance in science effected by they now well-known Dr. Williams, Mr. Elson was enabled to obtain a remedy altogether unlike ordinary medicine— Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. He says: "On the second day after taking them I felt my cough a little relieved. This encouraged me to go on with them. Before, I had had peace neither night nor day from my exhausting cough, but now I began to feel gradually better, and by the time I had had four boxes I was able to think of work. I recommenced my duties at Easter, and continued taking the Pills till I had finished two more boxes, making six in all. I have not found it necessary to use them regularly since, but keep a box by me in the event of my not feeling quite up to the mark"—Dr. Williams' Pills being of all tonic agents the most prompt and most certain in action. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are not a purgative, like other pills, but a strengthening nerve and blood tonic which can be taken without the least danger even by the most delicate, and are an unfailing cure for rheumatism sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, prostration, and all cases in which the system needs to be strengthened and built up. They are a specific for troubles of the female sex, and in men effect a radical cure in cases arising from mental worry, overwork, or excess of any kind. They are sold by chemists, or may be had direct from the Dr. Williams Medicine Company, 48, Holborn- viaduct, London, at 2. ed. a box, or six boxes for 13s. 9d. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are never sold loose, and the wooden box must be in a pink wrapper, bearing full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

SWIMMING GALA AT WARRINGTON.

A swimming gala was held at the Baths,Legb-st.• on Tuesday, under the auspices of the Warrington Swimming Club. The first part of the. programme ' was a members' race, for which there was a con- siderable number of entries. The officials were : Judge and handicapper,Mr.James Grace; check starters, Messrs. H. Toft and J. Beswick. The heats were well contested with the exception of the last, in which F. Garside had a walk or rather, a swim over. The final was a very even affair, the winners being—First, Fur- ness ; second, Ashton ; third, Garside. A grand display of ornamental swimming was then given by Master W. H. Curwen,the juvenile prodigy, who, although only thirteen years of age, has already won 31 prizes. He performed some most difficult feats, and he was deserv- ing of the applause be received. He appeared to be quite at home in the water. The first item on the programme was the most impor- tant,as it was the water polo match between Warrington and the Diamond T of Liverpool. These clubs had already met twice this season, the Warringtonians on each occasion proving the victors. The following were the teams w1,1,...h took part in the match on Tuesday night: —Warrington: J. T. Dixon, goal; E. A. Jones and S. Beaman, full backs; Turner (captain), half-backs; G. A. Scotson, T. Locker, and P. Furness, forwards. Dia- mond T : A. Gilbraith, goal ; M. Robinson and D.Jones, full backs; M. Jones, half-back; T. 134wt.r, W. B. Knowles (captain), and W. Williams, forwards. War- rington were fortunate in defending the shallow end in the first half. After desultory play, the Liverpool goal was invaded, and Scotson had hard lines in not getting something tangible. Several other shots from the War- rington men also went outside. At length Liverpool woke up, and Dixon had a very anxious time, but he at length cleared the danger, he at times making some splendid saves. Half-time arrived with nothing scored. The Liverpudlians had the worst of the second half, and they were almost the whole of the time kept defending, but bad shootir.g spoiled Warrington's chances, and full time arrived with still no score. Six minutes extra time was then played,and the first three minutes again found the teams level. In the second half of the extra time, however, Locker took a pass from Scotson, and banged it through the posts amid loud cheers. He re- peated the performance half a minute later, but as the time had expired, that goal was not counted, and War- rington retired victors by one goal to nil.

THE DUKE OF RUTLAND'S "BETTER HALF."

On Wednesday afternoon the Duke and Duchess of Rutland drove across the Derbyshire moors from Long- :thaw to Tideswell, where the Duchess opened a bazaar held to raise money for a new organ for the ancient parish church of 'I ideswell. The Duchess, in the course of an interesting speech, said she had lived a good deal among musicial people, and had observed the extra- ordinary and beneficial influence exerted by music over all classes. The Duke was also invited to address the gathering; but he exclaimed, "No, no; when the better half has spoken, the worse half remains silent," and amid much laughter resumed his seat.

A MUGII " WANTED " WOMAN AT

RUNCORN.

AN HOTEL PROPRIETOR TRACKS lIER DOWN.

At the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday after- noon, before Mr. F. J. Norman, an elderly and respectably attired woman, who has given her name as Ellen Turner, and wlio is known to have several aliases, was chatged in cu,tody on remand with stealing a pair of blankets, value 18s. from the Tem- perance Hotel, High-street, Runcorn, on the 4th inst., the property of James Lea. Margaret Sweetman, waitress at the hotel named, said that on Sunday, the End inst., she assisted her mistress to make a bed in the hotel upon which she put the blan- kets produced. On the following Monday evening the prisoner came in and ordered a bed. The one to which witness had just referred was allotted to her. In the morning, (Tuesday) witness let the accused out of the ,house but did not notice her take out anything except a cloak which was thrown carelessly over one of her arms. At about nine o'clock she went upstairs to the room which had been occupied by the prisoner and immedi- ately missed the blankets. She reported her loss to Mr. Lea who gave information to the police. Mrs. Julia Lea, wife of Mr. James Lea, proprietor of the Temperance Hotel, deposed to assisting the last witness to make the bed on Sunday night, and to the blankets (produced) being placed upon it. James Errington,assistant to Mr. Burton, pawnbroker, Foregate-st., Chester, stated that the accused entered the shop at 9.30 on Tuesday morning, the 4th inst., and asksd him to lend her 83. upon the blankets produced. He gave her 7s. upon them. Before accepting the articles he asked her if the blankets belonged to her, and she replied in the affirmative, giving" Ellen Whittingham " I as her name, and "Thornton Heath" as her residence. At about 12 o'clock the same morning witness received Information from the county police that a pair of blan- kets had been stolen, but be did not inform the con- stable who called that he hal that morning taken a pair into pledge. Being questioned by Inspector Hicks, he said the reason he did not tell the constable was that at I the time he was suffering from absent-mindedness, brought about by intense pain. The Inspector : Absence of mind in a pawnshop, eh P You had a minute description of the woman and blan- kets two hours after the woman had pawned them. The circumstance should have been fresh upon your memory. How many pairs had you taken in pawn that morning P Witness: I am an invalid and that accounts for a great deal of abgent-mindedness. The Inspector said that although the pawnbrokers had had a minute description of the woman and blankets given them by the police little more than two hours after the woman had pawned the blankets, yet when the constable called at Mr. Burton's shop, no informa- tion respecting them was given him. Had it not been that the accused had acknowledged that she had pawned them with Mr. Burton, they might have still been lying there. _ _ _ _ _ _ _

Witness explained that when the accused was in the shop he could not see more of her than he now could in the hex. He could not see then what dress sha wore. The Inspector had brought a serious accusation against him, and he should like his explanation entering upon the depositions. He could produce 50 pairs of blankets the next morning from the warehouses of Mr. Burton which would be found to bevery much like the ones pro- duced. He had been thirty years in the trade, and this was the first time his conduct had been so questioned. The Inspector : You are a very lucky fellow, and if you repeat such practices with the Runcorn police you will hear further of the matter. Witness : The Chester police are quite as strict as the Runcorn police, and they have always been satisfied. You, Mr. Inspector may make mistakes sometimes, but you may, perhaps, not care to say so. P.C. Richardson having given evidence, the accused, in answer to Mr. Ashton, magistrate's clerk, said that be , did not wish to say anything in reply to the charge. Mr. Lea, (speaking to the Bench) said that he bad heard the evidence given by his wife, and it was simply a corroboration of that given by the waitress. It would be very inconvenient if both of them had to be away from home at the same time. The hotel was now shut up owing to there being no one to look after it. If there had been required any evidence further than that of the servant he could have given more than his wife had done. _ _ _ -

Inspector Hicks observed that it was the dury øf the police to present such cases as this before the court in as impartial a manner as possible. Mr. Lea had not him- self seen the blankets put upon the bed and this evidence, as given by Mrs. Lea, was very material. Mr. Lea said he could have identified the blankets as well as his wife had done.

The Chairman intimated that as the police bore the responsibility of conducting the prosecution it would be unfair to interfere or curtail the number of their witneeses.

Mr. Lea repliad that he did not think prosecutors should be put to any unnecessary inconvenience. It was not likely to encourage people to put such cases in the hands of the police. He had explained the case to Superintendent Atherton and he had said that the presence of Mrs. Lea was not required. It was he (Mr. Lea) who went to Chester, found the prisoner and handed her over to the county police. The Inspector stated that the prisoner had only come out of gaol in June last after undergoing twelve months' imprisonment. She was wanted in Stockport, Old Trafford (for burglary) Ashton-under-Lyne, Warrington, and St. Helens.

The accused was eventually committed for trial at the next Knutsford Quarter Sessions.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

P.S.A. MEETING AT WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

There was wain a large attendance at the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society's meeting on Sunday afternoon. The chair was occupied by Mr. Wm. Beswick, who referred to the great benefit these meetings had been to many who had attended them. He thought the promoters of the movement should feel thankful that they had been the means of doing good to their fellow men. Mr.Pardy, of Openshaw, Manchester, delivered an interesting bible address. Mr. Furness ably recited "The Newsboy's Death." Mr. T. Wallington, secretary, announced that tlae membership had mu/ reached 1,001. SUNDAY AFTERNOON SERVICES FOR THE PEOPLE.

A new departure was made at the Bold-street Wes- leyan Chapel on Sunday afternoon, when a series of Sun- day afternoon services for the people was inaugurated. The services are on similar lines to the Pleasant Sunday Afternoon services held at Wycliffe and the Y.M.C. A., with the exception that women are allowed to be present as well as men. The programme consists of singing, prayers, addresses, and a soloist is also engaged. There is an excellent orchestral band under the conductorship of Mr. Pendlebury to lead the sing- ing. The opening service was very encouraging for the body of the chapel was well filled. The chair was occupied by the Rev. G. D. Mason, who, after the proceedings had been opened by singing and prayer,said , that he had been asked to state the object of their ser- vices. He said that he thought it was very evident that the movement had the sympathy of a large number of people. He wanted them to bs of a bright character, and such that husbands could bring their wives, and also both young men and young women could attend. Nothing would be spared to make all welcome. Mr. W. Hickman then addressed the meeting. He expressed his pleasure at seeing so many present at the inauguration of the movement. He had great sympathy with those kinds of services, as he had with all movements which , tended to the moral, social, and spiritual salvation of his fellow townsmen. They had commenced that afternoon under very favourable auspices, and they would be the means in the hands of God of doing much good. The , services must be bright in order to keep up the interest in them. Mr. Hickman then went on to give his experi- ene€s at P.S.A.'s in Birmingham. After the close of Mr. Hickman's speech a solo was very beautifully rendered, entitled" I love to hear the story," by Miss Rawlin. After further remarks from the Rev. G. D. Mason, the meeting was brought to a close by the pronouncing of the benediction.

OFFICES .-19e,Coleman-street, London,- and-Coed Talon,

Flintstiire. ' ABRIDGED PROSPECTUS.

This Company is formed to purchase the Coed Talon and the Black Diamond Cannel and Coal Leasehold Mineral Estates, together with the plant, machinery,rail- way sidings, mining rights, freehold land, houses, and other properties connected therewith, and to provide sufficient capital to develop and work the same. The two estates, which have hitherto been worked under separate management, adjoin and are situated in the well-known Leeswood Cannel District at Coed Talon, near Mold, Flintshire, North Wales. They comprise about 1,100 acres of valuable mining property, together with 13 acres of freehold surface estate, Leeswood New Hall, a manager's house, 60 workmen's cottages, and ranee of stalling. The business has hitherto been confined to working coal, but the particular object of this Company is to work the united properties so as to win as cheaply as possible the large quantity of valuable Cannel which has been proved under both Estates, and which, with the coal, is estimated to realise within 12 years a profit in working of over a quarter of a million sterling,as referred to in the Reports of Mr. John Higson,C.E.,M.E. (Messrs. J. and P. Higson), of Manchester, and Mr. William Lea, M.E., of Coed Talon, the former of whom is willing to accept the appointment of Consulting Engineer for working the Cannel. These Reports estimate that after one year's working, profits can be realised during the following ten years to pay dividends tor that period of over 20 per cent. per annum; and in addition to set aside a sufficient sum to redeem the Debentures and return the shareholders an amount equal to the entire capital now issued. The large profit derived from Cannel and its great commercial value is due to its demand for the purposes of manfacturing gas of a high standard of illuminating power, coupled with the decreasing supply in conse- quence of the rapid exhaustion of the properties now being worked. The London and North Western Railway runs through both properties, with large siding accommodation, and in connection with the Great Western Railway affords excellent communication, with Liverpool, Manchester, Birkenhead, and other parts of the kingdom. Mr. John Higson, C.E., M.E., of Manchester, states in his report on the Cannel underlying the Company's Coed Talon property : "The Coed Talon Coalfield is about the centre of the celebrated Leeswood Cannel Basin, and it is in this im- mediate district that the Leeswood Cannel has always been found to be richest and thickest. "I estimate that 12 months hence an average annual outturn of 50,000 tons of Cannel can be realised to endure for about 10 years at an average cost over that period of 7.. 6d. per ton, to be sold to realise, including Smooth and Curly Cannel, an estimated averave selling price of 153. 6d. per ton, or an income of £20,000 a year for 10 years." He also states in his Report on the Cannel underlying the Company's Black Diamond Property: "The Cannel Seam which possesses a section of 1 foot , 6 inches of coal and Seam, 6 inches of Cannel, may,after I deductions for faults, be reasonably taken as aforesaid to contain 90,720 tons, which ought to be worked at a cost of 7s. 6d. per ton, at the rate of 20,000-tons per annum, say 12 months hence, with an estimated average selling price of 15s. 6d. per ton to realise an income of £B,OOO for about 41 years." Mr. William Lea, Mining Engineer, who has been employed in the district thirty years,and for many years has acted as Consulting Engineer for these properties, states in his report: "The most important and valuable feature in your property is the valuable Cannel seams which I have proved under both estates, and which I estimate to contain nearly a million tons of Cannel of very good quality. "I consider you may calculate upon at least 103. per ton profit on the Cannel, and an average :,f about is. per ton on the coal." Professor Vivian B. Lewes, F.1.C.. F.C.S., of the Royal Naval College, Greenwich, and Gas Examiner for the Corporation of the City of London, who has recently visited the property, writes as follows : "I have examined your property at Coed Talon, and am pleated to inform you that I am perfectly satisfied with my inspection. "I consider that in consequence of the restricted and decreasing supply of good Cannel, coupled with the well- known quality and value of the same in your district. working your property should prove an extiemely remunerative investment for the shareholders."

The present selling price of Leeswood Curly Cannel is about 325. 6d. per ton, and of Smooth Cannel about 12s. per ton, whilst the average price for the last five years has been about 353. per ton for Curly Cannel and 123.6 d. per ton for Smooth Cannel. Taking these average prices (assuming there to be one-quarter Curly and three- quarters Smooth Cannel) the proportionate quantities would give an average profit of about 103. 6d. per ton, but the Directors are quite satisfied to bats° their calcu- lations upon Mr. Bigeon's estimate of an average profit over a period of ten years of only 83. per ton, which profit would be more than sufficient to provide for the I dividends and repayments as hereinafter stated. I Mr. Lea's Report, above referred to, shows that these properties also contain eight seams of coal, all of which have been proved and partially worked, and estimated by him to contain over twelve million tons of steam or house coal, of which a large quantity can be worked to advantage simultaneously with the Cannel,and states I that 3,000 tons of coal per week can be obtained from the Company's property within one year at an average profit of is. 6d. per ton, but inasmuch as the profit to be earned from the Connel alone should suffice to pay large returns on the entire capital of the Company the Directors are satisfied to base their estimate of profit from coal at the nominal figure of 6,1. per ton. On the basis of the foregoing reports the total net return over the period of 10 years would be as follows: From the Cannel underlying the Coed Talon Property.. £200,000 From the Cannel underlying the Black Diamond Property From the coal underlying both estates, at a I nominal profit of 6d. per ton I From freehold houses and land, £250 per annum (not mentioned in Reports)

£276,000 Equal to an average income of £27.600 per annum. After making an allowance to cover administration expenses, which the Directors considered should be ade- quate, the above amount would enable the Company to provide for the payment of interest and redemption of the debenture capital, to provide by the operation of a sinking fund for the return to the shareholders in the year 1906 of an amount equal to the entire capital now issued, and, in addition, to pay to them, after the first year's working, up to that time dividends at an average rate of over 20 per cent, per annum. The price to be paid to the vendors for the freehold and leasehold mineral estates, mining rights, plant, machinery, and other assets appertaining to the two above-mentioned properties is fixed by the vendors at the sum of £70,000, payable as to £37,500 in debentures and cash, and the balance in such manner as may be de- termined by the Directors. The full subscription for shares and debentures now' offered would provide, after completion of the purchase, a sum of £30,000 for development and working capital. A contract has been entered into on behalf of the Com- pany, dated the 2nd August, 1894, and made between the Black Diamond Coal and Canm!l Colliery (I..rnited), the vendors, of the one part, and A. THE revision of voters' lists for A Disqualified Councillor. Warrington has passed over in a rather prosaic fashion this year. There have been no particularly lively incidents, and none of the cases have raised any special principle or affected any large number of voters. Of course, it goes without saying that votes have been lost and won on the fine distinctions and legal technicalities which have to be taken into account under the present registration laws, and, altogether, the revision like every other revision provides conclusive arguments in favour of the registration reform, which the Liberal party is pledged to effect. Perhaps the most unfortunate of the claimants upon whom the Revising Barris- ter has adjudicated is Councillor MUIR. Mr. Mum seems to have had migratory tendencies during the past twelve months, with the result that his qualifying tenancy has been broken,and he has lost his right to vote and, also, we understand, to act as a member of the Town Council. This is awkward for Mr. Mum, as he will have no further opportunity of bringing a luminous intellect to bear on municipal affairs. Happily,from a public point of view his retirement from the To*n Council is a misfortune which can be repaired.

RUNCORN OCCASIONAL POLICE COURT.

On Wednesday afternoon at the Runcorn Occasional Police Court, before Mr. F.J. Norman, Patrick Wheelan, was fined ss. inclusive of costs for being drunk and in- capable on the previous evening in Mersey-street. In default of paying the fine he would be committed for seven days to gaol.

ANNIVERSARY SERVICES AT RUNCORN.

On Sunday last, the anniversary services in connection with the Camden Wesleyan Ctapel, Runcorn were con- ducted in the afternoon by the Rev. Wm. George, and in the evening by the Rev. T. Brackenbury. Able and instructive sermons were delivered on each occasion and the collection which reached a very satisfactory figure, were devoted to the trust funds. CHILD SCALDED AT RUNCORN.

On Monday evening, between five and six o'clock, Lucy Brown, aged three years, daughter of Wm. Brown, of 15, Cooper-st., received serious injuries from being scalded. he was playing in the kitchen, when she knocked a saucepan from the bar, and the boiling water ran over her neck, arms, and chest. Dr. Robinson was summoned, and he was soon in attendance. Under his care the little one is evidently making satisfactory pro- gress towards recovery.

CHURCH PARADE OF RUNCORN VOLUNTEERS. 1 he members of both Companies of of Runcorn Volun- teers assembled on Sunday morning at the Drill Hall, Runcorn, and preceded by the band, marched to the Parish Church. They were commanded by Colonel Ashton; Captain George Ashton also being present. The vicar, the Rev. Canon Wood, preached an appropriate sermon, subsequent to which they paraded back to the Drill Hall where, after being addressed by the Colonel, they disbanded. COLLIER'S is. BUTTER is the gem in the market.—Advt. RUNCORN BURIAL BOARD.

At the monthly meeting of the Runcorn Burial Board, held on Wednesday afternoon at the offices, Greenway- road, the secretary, Mr. Salkeld, presented his monthly statement. From this document it appears that for the five months ending 25th August, 118 interments had been registered compared with 235 in the corres- ponding period of the previous year, being a decrease of 117. The fees received during those months amounted to £123 4s. as against £205 6s. 10d. in the same period of last year, showing a decrease of £B2 2s. 10d. Tine PARAFFIN LAMP OUTRAGE AT WIDNES.

Before Alderman Timmis, on Monday, at Widnes, Catherine Mullen, Cromwell-st., was again charged with throwing a lighted paraffin lamp at Julia Maud M`Kay and doing grievous bodily harm.—lnspector Strickland said the injured woman was still in hospital, and would not be able to attend court within a. fortnight. —Mr. J. W. Knowles, who defended, applied for bail, stating that he understood the prosecutrix was not in danger.—Mr. Lewin (deputy magistrates' clerk) said that was not so, and Inspector Strickland opposing the application, the prisoner was remanded in custody till Monday next. RUNCORN CHORISTERS' PICNIC.

A jolly day was passed on Saturday by the choristers of Nelson-st. and Camden Chapels, Runcorn, when more than forty of them were conveyed in waggonettes by Messrs. Yarwood to Sutton Hall Farm, and hospitably enteitained by Messrs. Lowe. Various amusements were indulged in and were succeeded by an impromptu concert, Miss Temperley presiding at the piano. Sut- sequently tea was taken and was heartily enjoyed. Mr. and Mrs. Lowe were unanimously thanked for their hospitality by the choristers, aftea which, Mr. Lowe acknowledged the c@mpliment on behalf of himself and wife, expressing the pleasure such an entertainment gave both of them. The journey back to Runcorn was com- pleted in safety and good time.
