ACCIDENT ON THE SHIP CANAL AT RUNCORN.

The es. "Eva;" belonging to Messrs. Hessler and Co., of West Hartlepool, and carrying 800 tons of pulp wood, which had been imported by a local firm from Gefle, arrived in the Ship Canal on Sunday morning for Man- chester. She had a considerable" list" and was taken in tow at Eastham by the steam tug " Agnes Seed." At about 11.55, just after passing the Swing Bridge, Run- corn, she struck the right bank of the waterway between 66 and 67 chart. This was caused by the vessel, not immediately answering her helm. No damage was sus- tained, however, and the craft proceeded in perfect safety to Manchester. The official in charge of the Swing Bridge contradicts the statement that the vessel struck the buttres:: of that bridge and was considerably damaged. The point of the bank which she struck is quite one hundred yards from the swing bridge.

COLLIER'S BREAD 18 the best money can buy.—Advt. TWENTY-FIVE MILES RACE BY RUNCORN CYCLISTS.

In consequence of an objection being raised to certain members ot the Runcorn Cycling Club having pace- makers when competing in the last 25 mile race,the club members on Monday night at a meeting declared the 'race void, and decided that it should be run off again without pace-makers. Wednesday evening was chosen, and at the time appointed six competitors started. viz., George Whinyates, Charles Johnson, C. Tinsley, W. H. Mack, J. L. Withington, and J. Booth. The route lay through Stockton Heath, Grappenhall, past Mere corner near to Knutsford, thence to Bowdon, and Lymm, ani finished at High Legh. The roads were in very favour- able condition, and the riders were consequently able to make very smart time. The results were as follows : George Wllinyates, 1 hour 20 minutes; Charles Johnson, 1 hour 231 min. ; and C. Tinsley, 1 hour 27 min. Half a minute behind the latter came Mack ; twe minutes in th,! rear of him came Withington, but Booth's machine sustained a puncture, so that he was prevented from completing the distance. Mr. W. Rushton was the time- keeper and starter. The prizes to be awarded are three in number, and will be of gold and silver. COLLIER'S BLACK BLENDED TEA, 20. per quarter lb.—Advt. ANNIVERSARY OF THE Sr. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN SUNDAY SCHOOL, RUNCORN.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A RUNCORN_ TRADESMAN..

Soon alter Wedmesday midnight Mr. Richard Hamp- son, aged 61 years,tin and copper smith, died somewhat suddenly at his residence, 4, Waterloo-road, Runcorn. The deceased was widely known and highly respected, and his demise is generally mourned. His father carried on the business as a tin smith in the town, and brought his son up to the same trade. After serving his time he acted as foreman at a large works in Liverpool. Nearly 30 years ago he returned to Runcorn, where he cpened a shop in Church-street. Since then he had carried on a very successful business, especially in shipping circles, his manufactured articles being exceedingly well-known in that department. He was the first to introduce bul- lock horn fog horns, which for a time were greatly in demand. He also made marine lamps on an ex- tensive scale. For over a dozen years he, had the contract of the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners to supply and repair the lamps in their district. In politics he was a staunch Conserva- tive. He was a regular attendant at the Parish Church, and on Sunday evening was in his accustomed place. He was one of the orginators of the benefit and dividing club, held at the National Schools. which has proved so successful. This was the first institution of its kind in the town not connected with the publichouse. He was taken ill on Monday and despite the attentions of Dr. Mangan expired as stated above. He leaves a widow, a son, and two daughters, to whom sympathy is extended by an extensive circle of friends in their severe loss.

NEW CHEMICAL WORKS AT ST. HELENS.

A St. Helens correspondent understands that a chemical company is being formed, and that the negotiations are nearly concluded for the purchase of a works at Parr, St. Helens. The mast influential directors are Col. Holland and Mr. Walter, of the London "Times," while the general manager will be Mr. John Leith,a well-known St. Helens chemical manufacturer. The details of the proposed works are not yet obtainable, but bleaching powder and caustic soda will probably be manufactured. DEATH OF AN OLD WARRINGTONIAN.

On Tuesday the funeral took place at the Ceme- tery, of Mrs. Walter Ashton, the wife of Mr. Walter Ashton, wit-, was for some 20 yPars the manager of the Liverpool and District Bank, in Satikey-st. Mr. and Mrs. Ashton left Warrington about 20 years ago, and went to Sheffield. They afterwards left Sheffield and retired to outhport, where they have been living for some years. He was well known all over the county. Mrs. Ashton was 65 years of agE, and died on Friday last.

WOMEN AND GAMBLING.

The Dean of Manchester speaking on Thurclzy even- ing at a meeting in the Manchester Town Hall, said he had read with the deepest regret—with a feeling of al- most of positive consternation—that at the St. Leger there were two women bookmakers in the ring. What was wanted, he declared, was to make women better bookkeepers, better home-keepers, to make them useful in societv.and he denounced the idea that women should degrade themselves, as, he was afraid, was too often the case, whether they went into the ring or not, by stoop- ing to the awful curse, as he would call it, of betting and gambling.

DARING DIAMOND ROBBERY.

I daring robbery was committed on Thursday in Hatton Garden, London. Hearing that three men who had taken rooms there were purchasing diamonds for the Continental market, Mr. Spiers, a gem merchant, waited upon them with a parcel of precious stones worth £5,000. Late in the afternoon he was seen stag- gering across the roadway with his face blood-stained and his apparel torn. He said that on entering the house he was suddenly seized, a cloth saturated with chloroform was tied across his mouth, and after a des- perate struggle his parcel of diamonds was stolen.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCOTCH COAL STRIKE.

NO SETTLEMENT. A Conference of Miners' Delegates was held in Glas- gow on Thursday to consider the proposals of the non- associated masters for the settlement of the Scotch strike. The coalowners suggested that the men should return te work at the old rate of wages, and that a board of conciliation should be formed within six weeks- A resolution to that effect was proposed at the Confer- ence ; but an amendment was moved setting forth that the acceptance of the offer would be detrimental to the interests of the miners, and advising that no further action should be taken till the Federation Conference, which will be held at the end of this month. The amend. ment was carried by .53 votes to 28. Those who voted for the amendment represented 57.600 miners, and the delegates who voted against it 12,500. The Conterence was adjourned for a week. In the Glasgow iron market on Thursday buyers were eager in expectation' of the settlement of the dispute, but the rejection of the offer made by the non-associated coalowners to the pitmen again checked trade.

PROPOSED NEW COLLIERY COMPANY.—In OUT adver- tising columns will be found the prospectus of the United Cannel Collieries Limited. The share capital of the Company is to be £lOO,OOO, viz. : 75,000 shares Qf £1 each, and 500 debenture bonds of £5O each. The Coin- pany is formed for the purpose of purchasing the Coed Talon,and the Black Diamond Cannel and Coal leasehold mineral estates together with the plant, machinery. railway sidings, mining rights, freehold land, housesand other properties connected with them, and also capital for working the same. Previously the two estates have been worked under separate managemeut, and are situated in the Leeswood cannel district at Coed Talon, near Mold. North Wales. They comprise about 1,100 acres of valuable mining property in addition tol3 acres of freehold surface estate, Leeswood New Hall, a manager's house, 60 workmen's cottages, and a ranee of stabling. The business has hitherto been confined to working coal, but: the chief object of the Company is ta work the properties so as to Win the large quantity of cannel which has been proved under both estates. Together with the coal it is estim- ated to realise within twelve years a profit of ever a quarter of a million sterling. It is stated that the Lees- wood district has for many years yielded the finest des- cription of cannel in the marketaind that the large pr)fit derived from it is due to its demand for the purposes of manufacturing gas, and the decreasing supply in conse- quence ot the rapid oxhaustion of properties now being worked. The list of applications for sharps will close on or before Wednesday, September 19th, for London, and Thursday, the 20th inst., for the country.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR.

The tone of the Government Inspector's report concerning the Runcorn Board schools ought cer- tainly to create a sense of satisfaction in the minds of the members of the Board. it is reflective of the superior teaching capacity possessed by the staff employed by the Board, and the members of the stuff will also derive much gratification from the thought that their exertions have been crowned with such success. The highest possible grants were gained both by the Greenway-road Boys' School and Mill Brow Infants' School; whilst with the exception of two subjects this result was secured by the Greenway-road Girls' School. The first two schools were termed in the report as "ex- cellent," and the laq-named also received praise in many subjects. Therefore, the School Board, and teachers, and also the parents of the scholars ought to feel highly pleased with the reporc.

Anyone chancing to visit our local police court on a Monday morning will only too often hear youths and boys being charged with playing football, hoc- key and other games in the streets. Only last Monday there were instancesof this, and a harrow- ing tale was unfolded by the Inspector relative to the supposed damage sustained by private residents in consequence of lads indulging in exercises such as the ones cited. Twe ot the boys had been charged with playing football,but they persistently stated that they were only kicking an old hat about the street in which they resideci. Does it not seem a little hard on these juveniles that they should be so vigilantly watched by our "men in blue," and when discovered participating in such innocent fun for their names to be taken down in those horrid note-books and to be hauled before the local jus- tices the following Monday to answer for such misdeeds? I should be very slow to enter any pro- test whatever against this action on the part of the police if there was any place in Runcorn—any recreation ground—for the boys to assemble. But in a town where we have no such centres of amusement it seems a little rough that the harmless pleasure , for in many instances it is very harmless—of small boys should be so restricted what are the lads to do if they cannot play? We are a football-loving com- munity, and are proud of the teams that represent us. Of course youthful energy and skill are apt to be displayed in a rather uncomfortable manner to many folks, when they find that a street urchin throws them gently to Mother Earth by suddenly darting between their legs after a ban. But, after all,these occurrences are rare; and seeing that there are no places specially set apart in Runcorn for boys to indulge in a little recreation, this must necessarily take place in the streets to some extent; and it would not entail much physical suffering or damage to property it the police were to exercise a little mere leniency towards these boys than hitherto.

Runcornians are always pleased to hear of the success of any of their townsmen, and it may interest them probably to know that it is rumoured that Sir John Rigby is about to receive a judicial office, and that there will be consequently a vacancy in the representation of Forfarshire. Writing very recently a; London correspondent says "There is some difference of opinion as to what the office is to which the Attorney General is to be promoted. In one quarter it is said that he will succeed Lord Davey in the Court of Appeal, Mr. Justice Mathew's claims being ,postponed till the retirement of Lord Esher, whose position Sir John Rigby will then take. Another version is that the Master of the Rolls will retire before the law courts meet again, and that Sir John Rigby will succeed him. A third statement is that another of the Law Lordships will shortly be vacated, and that Sir John Rigby is destined for it. These discrepancies rather discredit the whole story. But the rumour that the Attorney General is about to be raised to some judicial post is receiving a good deal of credence in well-informed legal.circles."

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

I am disappointed, very much 60, that the Cycling Club have not yet made any move towards carrying out my suggestion for a cycle parade. I under- stand they have done some little towards it pri- vately,but they have not taken the public into their confidence. It is really a pity that the club should allow such a splendid opportunity of advertising themselves slip by, and not only this, but a deserv- ing object will lose a chance of being brought before the public in an interesting manner. The parade could do nothing but good, and as I understand the iclub has recently been soliciting subscriptions from the general public, their next appeal could be made with much greater confidence if they would show their willingness to aid a local charity. It is not too late even now, but the season is getting far advanced, and I believe in striking the iron while it is hot. Ido not know what the stumbling block is, but I don't think it is beyond removal.

The Commissioners have very graciously decided to return the property owners, that is those who live in their own houses, the deposit of 5. which they paid when first commencing to use the Com- missioners' gas. This is quite in order,and perfectly agreeable to me, but what I and all the gas con- sumers would like to hear is, when are the Com- missioners going to reduce the price of gas, or restore it to the price it stood at prior to the coal strike last year?. We were told when the price was advanced, that it was in consequence of the in- creased price of coal, and of course we did not grumble, as we thought it would only be of a tem- porary character. Evidently we were labouring under a delusion, for the coal strike ended nearly 12 months ago, and coal is as cheap as it ever was, and yet the price of our gas remains up. I don't suppose these comments will produce any effect, but I would just ask the ratepayers to remember this fact, when their votes are solicited for the district councillors.

Talking about district councillors I am afiaid there is going to be a nice kettle of fish shortly over this matter. I am disgusted, thoroughly so. Plitics ! Politics ! it is nothing else but politics when any little public job has to be transacted. Here we have to elect 15 men as district councillors slrntly, and yet we cannot keep politics out of it. It is very pleasant to relate that stiberals come out of this pure and white as snow. The sinners are the • Conservatives, and they are working with might and main to make the election a purely politi- cal one. Why they have even gone so far as to select their own candidates, for the various wards, and mind you they, are all Conservatives! But whether their candidates will meet with the approval of the ratepayers remains to be seen. I for one do not intend to have any selection from the Tory club foisted upon me. I think it is a piece of gross im- pertinence for any party to select candidates for wards in this way, withftut consulting the residents in the ward about the matter. I think when the time comes, they will find their cherished plans will require considerable alteration.

NO. 1 CONVEIITION, GOOD TEMPLARS

The No.l Convention of S. W. Lancashire, was held at the Co-operative Hall Earlestown on :7_atur- day. The Conventicn was called to order at 350 p.m. by Bro. W. H. Dale C.C.T. and there was a good attendance of officers. There was a large muster of deputies and representatives from Lodges in War- rington, Earlestown, St. Helens, Prescot, Ashton, Leigh The visitors present included Bro. James Cocks, P.G.A. secretary, Manchester, and Bro. Q. M. Sergeant T. Mantle, D.C.T. of Warrington Military dis- trict. The minutes of the last session having been read and adopted, the roll of officers was called, and the ap- pointment of Press and Credential Committees was pro- ceeded with. The reports of the officers were then re- ceived. Bro. W. H. Dale, C.C.T. reported that he had visited the " Ebenezer " and " Hope of Vulcan " lodges, and returned thanks for his election, asking for active support in carrying on the work. He expressed a hope ' to be able to visit the other lodges in the area during his term of office.when he trusted he would find them doing well. Bro. E. Newman C. secretary in his report stated ' that the Hindley Lodge which had been at a standstill for some time, had progressed somewhat. The Wigan lodge which had been closed, wal about to be re-opened. The "Hoge of Vulcan" lodge which had recently been opened had very good prospects, and the other lodges were in good conditions. He stated there was room for an extension of the order at Sutton,Collins Green, Edge Green, Lowton and Golborne. It was pointed out in discussion that there was no doubt need for extension, but the matter was allowed to lie over for a time. The report was adopted. Bro. J. Taylor, C.E.S., in his report alluded to the Brewster Sessions at War- rington, and commented upon the refusal Gf a license by the magistrates. The Credential Committee reported 65 present. An address of an earnest and encouraging character was afterwards given by Bro. Cocks and the , Convention closed with thanks to the Hope of Earles- town lodge for their arrangements. About 100 persons subsequently sat down to an excellent tea which was followed by a public meeting in the large hall. Bro. IV. H. Dale presided and he was supported by Bros. James Cocks, P.G.A. sec., Q.M. Sergeant T. Mantle, D.C.T., 3,:c. Bro. J. Cocks gave an address on temperance, in which he said that 26 years ago it was said that good temnlary would never live, but they could see that day that it lived, and there was no doctor of note to-day who was not on the side of temperance and teetotalism. He proceeded to quote the opinions of doctors and others upon the question of teetotalism and said if they wanted to be safe, they must keep away from the public-house. During the evening a good programme of instrumental selections, songs. recitations, Scc., was given by the fol- lowing, Bro. IV. IVillis, Sisters .13,1berts, Willis, J. 13a rcrof t, Travis and Warburton.

CONFERENCE OF REFOItMATORY MANAGERS AT NEWTON.

The biennial meeting of the Reformatory and Refuge Union (Manchester and Liverpool district), was held at the Liverpool Farm School, Newton, on Tuesday afternoon. Among those present were Messrs. Uzzell (Liverpool), Dixon (Blackburn). Wood, Young, and Day (Manchester), Blease and Cropper (Liverpool), Digweed, Miss Ross (Manches- ter), Miss Skinner (Liverpool), Miss Ferguson (Mount Vernon), J. S. Maddison (secretary), and Dr. Watkins.

After inspecting the school and buildings, with which the party expressed themselves as pleased, they partook of tea, and subsequently adjourned to the schoolroom where the meeting was held. The Rev. H. Monk pre- sided, and briefly introduced the subject Set down for discussion, which was " The Industrial Schools Acts (Amendment Acts), 1894." Mr. Maddison alluded in detail to the provision of the Act, and said that magistrates at one time, previous to the passing of the Industrial Schools Act, did not think that boys should be sent to a Reformatory instead of to prison. With regard to girls he did not think anybody entertained the view that they ought to be sent to prison before being sent to a reformatory. He spoke of che attempts which were made to get some such enact- ment passed, and he said the Bili was a very valuable one, as all interested in industrial schools would testify. The Amendment Act, he took it, was one of the most valuable additions, and the most useful in the reforma- tion of boys and girls of any Act passed during the last 20 years. Now they had got the Act the question was were they'going to use it? They had got more than they hoped for; it was retrospective in certain circumstances, and he should like to know if any of the managers of schovls had taken any steps to put it into force. He particularly referred to section 3, relating to the consent of a child to be detained in an industrial school. The question was what would satisfy the Secretary of State? In order to make the clause operative, the man- agers would have to testify that the matter was fully explained to the child before it would consent to be put under its operation. In this bill they had strengthened the licensing power, or control over children licensed. They had got the licensing power for two years after a child had been discharged, and they had strengthened those powers by making the penalties more sevele for anyone who assisted a child to escape. The question was what were they going to do with girls over 16 years of age who had left the Institution and forfeited their license? He found the greatest difficulty existed here. He thought they might take them back,and immediately license them out to a penitentiary. Mr. Cropper (Liverpool) said he had had large ex- perience in penitentiaries, and asserted they would not have the girls. Miss Skinner, of Liverpool, said when the girls knew they had the power, she thought the Act wo.:ld do a great deal for them. Mr. Cropper thought there was a great evil in the Act, because it gave them a great deal more anxiety. Mr. Maddison said it was a great responsibility in the hands of managers of schools to have this Act, but it was a great help, because they got an incentive to show an improvement. Mr. Wood said a case was brought up at their last meeting, where a girl of 16 did not want to go back to the penitentiary, and they suggested emigration., \ Mr. Maddison said they had a sort of new condition, which gave them to years of supervision, but this applied more particularly to industrial schools. The subject then dropped, the general opinion being that the Act was beneficial.

A BETTER LAND.

IS there a land, oh, canst thou tell, Where noble aspirations dwell, Free from all selfishness and care. That might with poet's dreams compare. Where no afflicted ever sigh. Nor the beloved can ever die; A tranquil state where all are blest, A land of sweet poetic rest. Where doubt, perplexity and grief, In knowledge find a full relief; A land from every evil free, When sin and sorrow cannot be. Beyond the grief of human life, Above Its vanity and strife, Beyond its joys that cannot stay, Where pleasures never pass away. Do happy spirits wing their flight To mightier realms undimmed by night, To peaceful spheres where all are blest, And radiant climes of heavenly rest. S. KENYON.

Warrington.

ST. MARY'S PAROCHIAL TRIP.—On Monday, the annual trip in connection with St. Mary's Church. took place. The place selected was Blackpool,and about 300 teachers, scholars, and friends took advantage of the opportunity to enjoy a trip to the popular watering place. Through the genenfsity of the Rev. E. S. Jackson, the vicar, the wives of the married choristers were taken free. After a tedious journey the party arrived at Talbot-road Station at 11-15 a.m. Dinner was served at the Palatine Hotel. Afterwards the party dispersed to the variow places of enjoyment. The party returned home at 10-30 having spent a very pleasant day. The Rev. E. S. Jackson and Rev. J. R. Jones accompanied the party.

REVISION COURTS.

WARRINGTON.

MONDAY.

The revision of votes for the borough of Warring- ton was opened at six o'clock on Monday evening by Mr. James T. Foard, at the Council Chamber of the Town Hall. The Liberals were represented by Mr. J. Clayton, and the Conservatives by Messrs. J. Hampson and W. Holbrook. The Town Clerk, who is away on his holidays, was represented by Mr. Staniey Wilson.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
AN INTERESTING CASE.

A claim for a lodger vote was made by Mr. James Grace, 47 Bewsey-street. This cl.tim was objected to by Mr. Clayton, on the ground that there were ten rooms in the house, and three other lodgers, and that therefore there was not sufficient value when divided. The rooms were only £l5 10s. net.—His Honour said that it was quite possible that the claimant had the best rooms in the house, and he did not see why they should not be worth 12s. a week with the furniture.—Mr. Clayton ; Next year we might have another claim from another lodger.—His Honour : Never mind about next year, the vote is all right tor the present, and I will allow it.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
A Y.M.C.A. LODGER.

Mr. John Martin Dale appeared to claim a lodger's vote for rooms in the Y.M.C.A. at Friar's Green. Mr. Tunstall (assistant overseeer) informed his Honour that the rooms were assessed at £5O gross. The claimant stated that he did not pay for fire, and had not had any for over twelve months. He occupied the room as a bedroom, and sat downstairs as a member of the associa- tion. He had the use of other rooms simply as a mem- ber.—His Honour held that that was in the nature of an easement with the rest of the premises, and that the use of the other rooms enabled him to dispense with the fire.—The claimant said that he had to pay for being a member.—His Honour, however, disallowed the claim.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
TOO MANY VOTES FOR ONE aorsE.

Mr. Edward Harold Price claimed a lodger vote for 43, Bewsey-street. Be said that he paid 12s. weekly.—Mr. Clayton informed his Honour that there were nine rooms in the house, and the value was £lB. There was another lodger already on the register for the same house, and Mrs. Brough, the occupier, had also a burgess vote.—His Honour said that as this was the case, the claim must be disallowed, as the law said that there must be £lO a year clear for each lodger vote.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
CLAIM BY A CHAPEL KEEPER.

Mrs. Isabella Broomhead claimed a vote for 15 St. Austin's-lane In reply to Mr. Hampson. she stated that she held the position of servant at the chapel house of the Friars Green chapel. She did not pay the rent, but worked for it.—Mr. Clayton submitted that the house was not in any way part of the chapel, as it was not under the same roof.—His Honour held that claimant had to be on the spot.and therefore he must disallow the claim.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

The hearing of claims and objections for Town Fall, Bewsey, Whitecross,and Orford Wards was continued on Tuesday mornin2. COUNCILLOR SHAW'S STEPSON.

Councillor Shaw applied for a municipal vote for his stepson, Walter Longshaw, in respect of a workshop in Dallam-lane. He stated that Mr. Longshaw was the sole occupier and the tenant of the workshop, and the dwel- ling-house was not connected with it. Mr. Dingshaw was a plumber, and he (Mr. Shaw) was a builder, although originally he was a plumber. Three years ago he turned over a portion of his business to his stepson who managed that part on his own behalf.—The vete was allowed.

'AN ANNUAL APPLICATION."

Councillor Shaw also applied for a lodger vote for his stepson for rooms at 89 Dallam-lane.—Mr. Clayton : This is an annual application, and your Honour dis- allowed it last year. The house in question is a public- house.—Councilor Shaw said that the claimant occupied two rooms separate from the family.—Mr. Clayton : But his meals are served up with the family.—Councillor Shaw : He simply comes into the kitchen when we are having our meals, and has them with us.—The vote was not allowed.

NO SUCCESSION FROM A LODGER TO AN OCCUPIER'S VOTE.

Mr. John Henry Halton applied for an occupier's vote for 73 Lively-lane.—ln reply to Mr. Clayton, Mr. Halton said that last year he claimed a lcdger's vote. His mother died on September 18:h. She was tenant of the house, although h • was practically so.—His Honour: You appeared as a lodger last year. There is no transfer from the lodger franchise to the household franchise. Had you claimed a municipal vote.l might have granted it as successor to your mother. You will, however, be entitled to a vote next year.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
A TOWN COUN-CILLOR LOSES MS . VOTE.

Councillor- James Muir appeared to make a reclaim for premises occupied by him at 55 Bewsey-street. In reply to Mr. Clayton, Mr. Muir said that he left 56 Bewsey- street on July 26, and went to reside at Stockton Heath with his son for a considerable time. During that time he placed a card in the window advertising it to be let, although it was not let. He came from Stockton Heath on November 20, and went to reside at his own house.— Mr. Clayton : Did you not reside at 3 Tnynue-street for a short time.—Mr. Muir , Yes.—Did you not become tke owning tenant :—I paid the rates.—Did you not give notice that the gasbill should be made in your name suppose so. In reply to further questions,Mr. Muir said that he had vaid the rates for the house in Bewsey-street the whole of the year —His Honour : You went into an- other house and became tenant, and lam afraid that this would be fatal.—Mr. Hampson ; He went to 3 Thynne-street, simply for his own convenience.—His Honour : He must keep an unbroken tenancy in one house. I must therefore disallow the vote.—At the close of the proceedings, Mr. Hampson again asked His Honour to reconsider the matter as Mr. Muir was not the actual tenant of 3 Thynne-street. His Honour how- ever declined to do so, and as a result, Mr. Muir will be disoualified to sit in the Council after November next.

WEDNESDAY. CLAIMANTS FROM TRE BARRACKS.

The first cases heard were on behalf of three officers from Orford Barracks, namely Messrs. Humphrey Woolrych, Charles Sutton Eldridge, and John Lee Kirk. The applicants were represented by Sergeant Major Rice. In answer to Mr. Clayton, the Sergeant baid he could not state whether Mr. Woolrych residei at Penketh.— Mr. Clayton: He does to all intents and purposes.—Mr. Hampson : But he is on duty at the Barracks daily.— Mr. Foard : There is no inhabitancy.—Mr. Sampson : He occupies during the day. The vote was disallowed. —ln the case of Mr. Eldridge it appearad that that gen- tleman resided at No. 9of the officers' quarters and the vote was allowed. In answer to Mr. Clayton, the Ser- geant Major said the servant% did not sleep in the officers' quarters. Mr. Kirk's vote was also allowed. Sergeant Major Rice re-introduced Mr. Wonlrych's case but the Revising Barrister told him that although there was no vote for the Barracks, he was no doubt entitled to one for Penketh.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
AN INDIGNANT CLAIMANT.

A claim was made by Mr. James Cunliffe, 12. Ship- yard, for a householder's vote. Mrs. Cunliffe appeared and the Revising Barrister having granted the vote,Mrs. Cunliffe asked for her expenses as she had lost consider- able time in attending the court. His Honour explained that as it was a claim, he could not allow any expenses. This caused Mrs. Cunliffe to become very indignant and she exclaimed as she left the court: "Not another vote will be claimed here by me."

===== 0002601_18940915_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. JOHN WHITE'S NEPHEW CLAIMS A VOTE.

Mr. John Eustance Seeker, Barrvfleld. Latchford. the nephew of Mr. John White,J.P., claimed a lodgers' vote. He stated that he occupied a bedroom himself. He had his meals with the family and there were twelve rooms in the house.—His Honour : What is the rateable value of the house P—Mr. Tunstall : gros3.—Mr. Clayton submitted that there was not sufficient value for a vote but his Honour said that they could not put the attics upon a level in value with the rooms on the first floor for instance. The claim would ht., allowed.

HIS WIFE'S NAME ON THE RENT BOOK. A claim was made by Mr. Wm. Barrett, for premises 48, Hopwood-street. In reply to Mr. Clayton, Mr. Barrett stated that his wife's name was on the rent book. The claim was disallowed.

EXPENSES PAID. An objection was taken by the Liberals to the vote of Mr. John Robinson, a police constable residing at 3, Challinor-St. In reply to Mr. Clayton, he stated that he left Ezerton-st. for his present residence on November 13. The rent book of Egerton-st. was in his mother's name, but she had died since then. He had been the sole support of his mother. The objection was dis- allowed,and the Revising Barrister ordered Mr. Robinson to receive 35. 61 as his expenses for loss of time, this being the first and only expenses allowed during the present revision.

TEIURSDAY.

The work of Thursday was entirely confined to starr- ing and going over the lists. It might be mentioned that Father Craven's name was struck off, on account of joint tenancy of a dwelling-house with Father Cody, as it does not come within the statute. Several others were also struck out for the same reason.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTES OF THANKS.

At the close of the revision, Mr. Hampson thanked the Revising Barrister ff,r the courtesy and fairness he had shown throughout the revision. That was probably the last time he (Mr. Hampson) would appear before hitn. tor he had retired and ;Tone to live in the country. Mr. Clayton, on behalf of the Liberals, also thanked Mr. Foard.

Mr. Foard, in reply, thanked each gentlemen for their good wishes. He was sorry to hear Mr. Hampson was going to leave them altogether, although he was glad to find that he was in that position as to be able to retire and live upon his laurels as a country gentleman. (Laughter.) He was glad on that account, that he had found a much more profitable living than many other people, himself (Mr. Foard) included. He hoped, how- ever, to see Mr. flampson again at sane future time. Mr. Hampson and Mr:Clayton also thought that the overseers ought to be thanked for the manner in which I they had drawn ula the lists. The proceedings then terminated.

ST. HELENS.

I At the St. Helens Town Hall, on Monday, Mr. J. T. Foard continued the court for the revision of voters for the Parliamentary and municipal borough of Sr. Helens. The lists for North Eccleston and South Eccleston Wards were taken. Mr. J. P. Mearns and Mr. Bain again re- presented the Liberals, and Mr. J. O. Swift and Mr. Lamb the Canservatives.—Mr. Andrew Muir, the assist.. taut Liberal agent,was objected to by the Conservatives on the ground that he was not the tenant of 4, Bank-st. Mr. Muir said he had been the tenant for nearly six years, and produced a certificate from the landlord.— The objection was overruled, and the vote allowed—Mr. Muir: May I have my expenses? It is a very frivol- ous and vexatious objection.—The Revising Barrister : What is the nature of the objection P—Mr. Lamb: That he has not occupied.—Mr. Muir was allowed 35. 6d. ex- penses. _ _ _

The Liberals objected to the name of John Corrister, of 115, Lyon-st.—Mr. Swift said it was apparently a printer's error. They were agreed that the name was not Corrister, but Collister. It was utterly absurd to to deprive a man of the franchise under those circum- stances.—The Revising Barrister said the man should certainly take the natural precaution. He had got notice, and the law said the objection was fatal unless the person objected to came to the court or corrected the name by declaration.-51r. Swift submitted that un- der sub-section 10 of section 28 of the Act of 1878 printa-facie evidence must be given by the objector un- less he was an overseer. The section set forth that unless such proof was given to the satisfaction of the revising barrister the name of the person objected to should be retamed.—Mr. Mearns Said the man was not present, and he did not take the trouble to correct it.— Mr. Swift : We don't ask for proof where it is a satis- factory objection.—The Revising Barrister said he did not think Mr. Swift doubted that the man had received notice, and that was very strong prima Jane evidence against him.—Mr. Twist (overseer) said they had the name Corrister on the, survey.—Mr. Mearns : If Mr. Swift insists on his right to have evidence we will give it.—Mr. Swift: I would be ashamed to make such an objection.—The Revising Barrister: Of course the ob- jection may be a frivolous one, but if the name is incor- rectly spelled a declaration should be made. I will,how- ever, alter the name to John Collister, and allow it to tand.

FRODSHA M. The revision of the list of voters for the Eddisbury division was begun on Tuesday by Mr. Burke Wood, barrister-at-law, at the Railway Inn, Dunham-o'th-Hiil. Messrs. W. Handley and B. Kniveton represented the Liberals, and Mr. R. N. Owen the Conservatives. There were no cases containing interesting points at this sitting and the court was subsequently adjourned to the "Bear's Paw," Froifisham. The Revising Barrister remarked that in the Wirral division there was a large number of claims for single women as parochial electors. A common instance was that of a school mistress. It seemed rather hard that while an ill-conditioned brute of a woman might be slipped on the parochial electors' list, a respect- able and educated woman in charge of a school could not be put on. The Liberal agents here sustained one ownership claim and the Conservative agent made two ownership claims for Kin,gswood on behalf of Mr. Henry Yarwood, Greenway-road, Runcorn, and Frederick Yar- wood, Albert-st., Runcorn, the claimants being repre- sented as the joint owners of three freehold fields in the township of Kingswood. Mr. W. Handley complained of an irregularity on the part of the overseer, inasmuch as that gentleman had detained the claims until after the date for raising any objection.—The claims were allowed. In Kingsley there were three ownership claims, to which there were no objections. Several claims were made and sustained for lodgers at Frodsham, among them being one by Henry Nugent Linaker, residing with Mr. Arthur Linaker at Mill Bank, one by Edgar Harold Hamleyn, lodging with Mr. James Reynolds, Main-street; and a third by George Aston, who lived with his mother. The Liberals also success- fully claimed a place on the list for Richard Stevenson Henshall, living with his parents at the Commercial Inn, Main-street. A claim by the Conservative agent for Ernest Price, who occupied rooms at the house of Mrs. Holland, was not allowed on account of the payment made being insufficient.—Claims were also allowed on behalf of William Simin, Alvanley-terrace, Frodsham I Lordship, and the Rev. W. T. Dickenson, the Vicarage, , Overton.

ST. HELENS

LICENCES FOR FOOTBALL MATCHES.

At St. Helens on Tuesday, Mr. Liptrot applied for and was granted a temporary authori'y to sell intoxicating liquors on the ground of the St Helens Football Club, at Knowsley-road,from 3.30 to 6.30 on Saturday next,when the Saints play Ulverstou.

A PRESCOT DISTURBANCE. At St. Helens County &ssions on Tuesday, Bridget Murray, of Prescut, who apneared with her head bandaged, charged a neighbour named Ann Kelly with having assaulted her. She alleged that on Saturday night Mrs. Kelly attacked her and struck her on the head with a jar, remarking that Mrs. Murray had sent her for three months, but it would be eighteen months next, time. Mrs. Kelly denied the assault, and called witnesses who stated that it was Mrs. Murray's son who indicted the injury.—Mrs. Kelly was bound over to keep the peace for three months.—A summons against Mrs. Murray for breach of the peace taken out by Mrs. Kelly's daughter was adjourned for a week.

THE GAMBLE INSTITUTE.

The ceremony of laying the foundation stone of the free library and technical school which Colonel D. Gamble, C. 8., has presented to the borough of St. Helens, has been definitely fixed for Tuesday, the 2nd October. The buildings are being erected at the corner of Hardshaw and Corporation Streets, near the Town Hall, and the total cost will be over £20,000. The foundation,or memorial stone,will be laid by the donor, Colonel Gamble, and the interesting ceremony will be followed by a banquet to the members of the Corpora- tion, on the invitation of the Mayor (Alderman A. Sin- clair). In the evening his Worship will hold an "At Home "at the Town Hall.

THE PROPOSED NEW BATES. At the monthly meeting of the St. Helens Water Com- mittee on Wednesday, Alderman J. M'Bryde presidingsa letter was read from the Local Government Board stating that they had had under consideration the report made by their inspector. Colonel Basted, after the inquiry held by him with reference to an application by the corporation for sanction to borrow £3,000 for the Erec- tion of public baths at Sutton. Regarding the taths, the board were advised by their chief engineering inspec- tor that the drainage arranagements, as at present designed, were not such RS could be approved, an d that it was desirable that the corporation engineer should attend at the Local Government Board office in London to confer with the chief engineering inspector on the sub- `ject. There were other details also, as to which the , suggested conference might prove useful. The com- mittee decided that the matter should remain in abey- ance for the present.

Sr. HELENS TRAMWAYS. The half-yearly report of the directors of the St. Helens and District Tramways Company for the six months ending July 31st, 1894, was issued on Saturday. The directors are pleased that the general manager, Mr. Cutler, has, by close attention to rolling stock, .main- tained a satisfactory service of cars, aad there Is evi- dence of a return of more prosperous times, as indicated by the increase in the company's receipts. The dir;:ctors do not anticipate a repetition of the disastrous coal strike ot last year, and, considering the general depres- sion of trade during the last half-year, they consider the traffic receipts most satisfactory and encouraging far the future. A portion of the tramway has heen relaid with new girder rail at a cost of £1,150, and the portion done is giving great satisfaction. The directors recommend a dividend at the rate of 5 per cent, on preference shares and 2i per cent. on ordinary shares. The preference dividend will absorb £163 7s. 6d., and is provided for in the balance-sheet. The dividend in the ordinary shares will absorb £203 183. 21. from the balance of £1,900 2s. 51q1. The net balance of £1,696 4s. 311. the directors pronose to deal with as follows :—To early £1.5)0 to the credit of a reserve fund, and £196 41. 31i. forward to the next half-year's aeenunt. The miles run during the • half-year were 83,102, and the passengers carried 681.718. The balance-sheet shows that from traffic receipts £4,529 53. ld, had been received.aeainst £4,579 16s. 11d, in the corresponding half of 1893,'a decrease of £5O us. 10d., but the traffic wages showed a decrease of £66 103. 3d., the totals bring £l,OOB as. 2d., against £1,074 19s, sd. in 1893. HEALTH COMMITTEE AND SEWERING EXTENSION. The monthly meeting of the Health Committee of the St. Helens Corporation. was held on Wednesday, Coun- cillor J.Forster presiding. The Town Clerk reported that the LocalOwernment Board had given their formal sanc- tion to the borrowing by the Corporation of £1,570 and £4,430 for works of sewage, including the area of the ex- tension of the borough. The first sum represented an amount which it was estimated would be recovered from owners of property in the Denton's Green district. The Board recommended that the proposed 24-inch pipe line from Windle City should he of brick instead of earthen- ware, and also that in the construction of the storm water sewer a bed of six inches of concrete should be laid where the ground was had.—The Chairman said that the extra expense would have to be borne mainly by the corporation. If they had to put in an expensive sewer, the Corporation's share of the cost would be rather heavy He suggested that the surveyor report to the next meet- ing with regard to the difference in the cost. The Chair- man's suggestion was agreed to.—The Town Clerk said the Board had also given their sanction to the loan of £2,750 for the erection of a public abattoir. The Sur- veyor was -instructed to take out quantities.—The Medical Officer, reported on the health of the borough for the past seven weeks, stating that during the whole of that period the general health of the town bad been most satisfactory. The total births werb 364, and the deaths 179, giving a death rate of 17 07 per 1,000, against 26.3 pe? 1,000 for the same period last year. The rate was certainly the lowest on record for this time of the year. There had been 35 deaths fryin zymotic. disemes, against 62, and 71 notificaticne of mktg.'s&

A VALUABLE DISCOVERY.

Most readers have had some experience of sick rooms, vither as patients or attendants. How many such are there who have not felt the weakness or debility which follows the sickness, more discouraging than even the disease itself ? Though the disease may have given way before the timely application of proper remedies, and though, in consequence, no immediate danger is to be apprehended, the slow progress towards convalescence is very disheaztening to patient and attendant alike.

How °ltem do we hear people say :—" Yes. The fever has left him. He is free from any immediate danger. But he is so weak. If he could only regain strength !" This debilitating effect of fevers and other diseases is indeed a great source of anxiety to medical men as well as to their patients; and anything which would tend directly to shorten the period between the departure of the fever and the complete recovery of strength would be naturally welcomed by both doctors and patients.

This is one of the reascns why Gwylym Evans' Quinine Bitters are so popular,why doctors prescribe it so largely, why chemists sell it so generally, why patients tave recourse to it so readily, and why everybody =as good word to say for it. Not only does it attack the disease in its stronghold, not only does it drive the evil from the system, but it gives hack directly the strength f which the system has been deprived by sickness ; it infuses new life into the blood, and the recovery of tealth and strength, instead of being, as it otherwise would be, discouragingly slow, intermittent, and uncer- tain, becomes, when this excellent medicine is resorted to, encouragingly rapid, regular, and certain. It is, therefore, confidently recommended as an unrivalled tonic medicine,and may he safely used by all who suffer from any form of weakness or debility from whatever cause it may arise.

August were dealt with in a similar manner. PLAYING HOCKEY IN THE STREETS.—Two boys named Robert and John Boyle were summoned for playing hockey in Regent-street.—P.C. Butler gave evidence which showed that the defendants were outside their homes at about 3.20 p.m. on the 4th inst. playing at the game mentioned. He had warned them to cease about half-an-hour I.reviously.—lnspector Hicks complained of the nuisance created by boys such as the defendants , who, he said, caused much mischief by their playing.— ; The defendants were discharged upon payment Of costs, 4s. 6.1. each. FOOTBALL IN THE STREETS.—Joseph Finlow (14), Wm. Lever (15), and John Ball, the last of whom did not appear, were summoned for playing football in Cooper- street, on the 2nd September.—P.C. Butler supported the charge, but the defendants said they were only kick- ing an old hat about the street. Some other boys, how- ever, had been in the street previously playing with a ball.—The mother ot the boy Ball appeared in court cry- ing and stated that since Saturday last she had neither seen nor heard anything of her son. He had disappeared from home,and she had not the least idea where he was. She expressed her willingness to pay his fine if the Bench would allow her.—Finlow and Lever were ordered to pay the cests,and the case against Ball was adjourned for his attendance. A CYCLIST IN TROUBLE.—Henry Lightfoot, of Heath- road, was fined 103. and Bs. 6.1. costs for furiously riding a bicycle in High-street on the 31st ult.—P.C. Butler stated the circumstances of the case and expressed the conviction that the defendant was riding at the rate of 10 miles an hour down High-street. A DESTRUCTIVE Boy.—John Alfred Bennett, 13 years of age, pleaded guilty to committing damage to the extent of 23. 61 to property belonging to the Manchester 1 Ship Canal'Comp.any, at Runcorn, on the 30th ult.—He was ordered to pay the costs 183. 6d. or in default seven days imprisonment.

ADJOURNED LICENSING SESSIONS.

- ST. HELENS. A SATISFACTORY STATE OF AFFAIRS.

The adjourned annual licensing sessions for the borough of S. Helens were held at the Police Court on Tuesday, the Mayor (Alderman Sinclair) presided, the other magistrates present being Colonel Gamble. C. 8., Alderman T. Cook, Councillor J. Foster, Messrs. W. Gamble, B. A. Dromgoole. 1. Glover, and J. Cotton. Mr. J. 0. Swift applied on behalf of Francis Cunning- ham for the renewal to the Star Inn, Merton Bank-road. When the temporary authority was granted the bcnch suggested that the house had not been conducted satis- factorily, and that prior to the transfer being granted they would require evidence of its being conducted better. The Chief Constable said the applicant had con- ducted the house satisfactorily. The licence was granted. Licences in place of temporary authorities were also granted to William Lythgoe.of the Red Lion Inn, Robins- lane: Catherine Barrow, New Inn, L iverpool-street ; and Matthias Goulding, York Hotel, Nutgrove. There was only one house mentioned in the black list, and that was the Horse Shoe, Higher Parr-street. A new tenant in the person of William Kay,through Mr. Swift, applied flr a renewal, and the magistrates, after urging the proper conduct of the house granted the application. The magistrates agreed to allow the new house called the Stanley Inn, Peasley Cross, to be occupied as soon as it was completed. The Mayor commented upon the de- crease of drunkenness in the borough, and said that had been the most satisfactory annual licensing sessions they had had.

Miss Daisy Bowie, of Newcastle-upon-Tyne, has csrried off the BPlgian literary distinction known as the Breve de Capacite, awarded by the Brussels authorities.

WARRINGTON A v. BARTON A.

There was a large number of spectators on the Wil- derspnol enclosure on Saturday afternoon to witness the match between these two clubs. Contrary to expecta- tion Barton were only represented by their A team, the first team being engaged at Aspull, and consequently the g..me was of a more one-sided nature. Barton kicked off towards the pavilion goal but a smart return by the Warrineton players carried the ball into the visitors territory. The two teams now settled down to work, and for s'ome time neither side seemed to have the advantarre. One noticeable feature at this point of the game was the unsuccessful, though well-attempted drop at goal 113 Alannion. Warrington continued to play up with vigour and at length their efforts were successful, Creswell being the first to score. The kick at goal failed. The superiority of the Warrington team wrts soon apparent, their passing and combination being far and away better than their opponents. Three additional tries were scored in quick sucQeFsion by Warrington players, viz. : Fairhur,t, Lawless, ani Webster. The only disappointment from the spectators' point of view wps the miserable attempts made by Warrington men to co'ivert them into goals. At the sound of the whistle for half time the score stood, Warrington four tries, three minors, Barton. nil. The second half of the game was perhapi more one-sided than the first, the visitors rarely entering the Warrington quarters. For the home team Webster and Lawless were notable players, the former in particular doing useful service by his " strong" runs. The Warrington score 'was augmented into the formidable score of two goals, five tries, three minors, before "no side " was called. The visitors were unable to score at all, and seemed more a match for the Warrington second team.

time he started the game before 5,000 spectators. Flowers returned,and Widnes were beaten in the ensuing scrimmages. Alford was grassed near the line, and things looked ominous for the hotnesters until Barber brought relief by some smart play. Some fine combina- tion by the Wigan three-quarters was spoiled by Alford. Widnes gradually worked their way into the Wigan 25, and an exciting scrimmage took place on the visitors' line, but rash passing among the backs spoiled a good opportunit7, and Wigan were again on the aggressive but Rispan and Clarke transferred the play for a short time. A combined rush by the visiting forwards caught the Widw>s defence napping. Webster intercepted a pass of Alford's, and passing to Flowers that player scored at the corner flag after fifteen minutes' play. Winstanley failed at goal. After some midfield play Webster took a free kick on the centre line, and nearly landed a goal. Wigan continued to hold the upper hand their heavy forwards carrying most of the scrimmages. The SVidnes lines were several times in danger, but the backs did their work well, and prevented further score. Half-titne : Wigan 1 try, Widnes nil. Webster restarted, and Wigan were soon on the attack,l4ut nothing resulted and a futile fusilade was made on the Wigan citadel. The play of the homesters was much smarter in this half, and they did most of the pressing, but the Wigan defence was very sure. Final result: Wigan 1 try ; Widnes nil.

ASSOCIA.TION. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. SEeOND DIVISION.

WINWICK V. ORFORD.—Played at Winwick, the home team being a strong one. Half-time score: Winwick, 1 goal; Orford, 0. Final: Winwick, 6 goals ; Orford, 0. WARRINGTON Sr. MARY'S V. BASEBALL (RESERVE).— Played at Gorsey-lane on Saturday on tbe ground of the latter, and resulted in a win for the visitors. W. O'Neil and E. Rigby were the scorers for St. Mary's, while McGowan scored for the Baseball. Final result :—St. Mary's, 2 goals; Warrington Baseball, 1 goal. PETER WALKER AND SON V. WARRINGTON BASEBALL.— Played on Saturday OR the Brewery ground. In the first half, the homesters, playing with the wind in their favour, pressed continuously. Scotson scoring the first goal after about twenty minutes play. The visitors played up much better during the second half, and were several times near scoring. A nice piece of passing by the Brewery forwards enabled Gregory to add to the score. Final result: Peter Walker and Son, 2 goals ; Baseball. nil. LANCASHIRE ALLIANCE. -

EARLESTOWN V. PARK-LANE WANDERERS.—EarIPStOWR having j Ined the above Alliance, they played their first fixture on Saturday afternoon with Park-lane Wanderers at Esrlestown. Both teams were well represented, and there was a fair number of spectators. The visitors won the toss, and elected to play with the hill and the wind in their favour. Earlestown kicked off, and the visitors at once began to press, a good attempt at goal just razing the cross bar. Earlestown cleared, and for a short time the game was of a give and take character. With the assistance of the wind, the Wanderers again pressed, and with excellent play had another shot at the Earlestown goal, which however went wide. Earlestown now took up the pressing and the Wanderers goal was in jeopardy, but Topping's shot flew wide. The Wan- derers next visited the home goal,but Wilkinson cleared, the attack was again taken up, and in attempting to clear, Wilkinson headed the ball through his own goal From the kick-off a fine bit of play on the part of the Earlestown forwards brought the ball to the Wanderers goal, but Burke misjudged with his head. The ball was afterwards worked down to the Earlestown goal, and the Wanderers mulled two easy chances in succession, just beneath the home cross bar. At half-time the score stood Wanderers one goal, Earlestown nil. On the change of ends, Earlestown had the advantage of the wind, and at once took up the pressing, and made 1 several attempti at the Wanderers gal, but without suc- cesss. The visitors repelled all attempts at scoring,and for a shcrt time pressed somewhat. Visits were after- wards paid to each goal, and ultimately Earlestown suc- ceeded in equalising, time being called with the score a goal each.

THE INDEPENDENT LABOUR PART'S.

MEETING AT WARRINGTON.

A well-attended meeting was held in the Co- operative Hall, Sankey-st., on Saturday evening under the auspices of the newly-formed Warrington branch of the Independent Labour Party. The chair was occupied by Mr. John Matthews (Warrington), and there were also on the platform Messrs. T. Fox (Stalybridge), Charles Gregory (Manchester), J. Svvindlehurst (Neston), Lees (Oldham), T. Malkin (President of the Warrington Trades Council), and Bullock (secretary Warrington Trades Council). • The CHAIRMAN apologised for the absence of Mr. Leonard Hall, Labour candidate for N.E. Manchester, who was unable to be present owing to the Trades Union Congress at Norwich not having concluded. He (the Chairman) felt that it was necessary that the work- ing people themselves should have a party of their own to do their own work in their own way. Their motto was, as Independent Labour men, "Government of the people by the people, for the people." Mr. T. Fox, in comparing the two great political parties of the present day, stated that the Conservative party consisted chiefly of landlords, and the Liberal party were mostly capitalists. He asked how they could expect such men to rule beneficially for the working classes? He contended that they had governed very well for themselves. (Laughter.) It was time tor working men to have their own representatives who would look after their interests. The speaker referred to the development of machinery, the large number i of unemployed, and the condition of people living in slums. He contrasted it with the condition of the people 150 years ago, when the working people said the articles they themselves had made and got full value for the work they had done. Tile capitalists, he affirmed, just gave sufficient wages to keep their work- people alive, in order that they might earn money for them. Speaking of the Independent Labour Party, he said they were determined that with the help of the people of England, they would nationalise the land and the means of production. (Applause.) Four-fifths of the electorate were working-people, and if they desired better conditions they had the power in their hands to bring them about by meang of the ballot. Neither Liberal nor Tory could do it for them without curtailing their own privileges. (Hear,hear.) He asked the meet- ing to support the Independent Labour Party. Mr. CHARLES GREGORY said they were there that night on a mission, and that mission was to teach the gospel of democracy. They spoke of their democratic govern- ment but how could it be so when the working classes were cnly able to claim twelve men in a Parliament of over 600 members? They were entitled to four-fifths of the le presentatives in the House of Commons, and yet at the present time one-fifth was able to claim 600 repre- sentatives. It was time for workingmen to be "up and doing" as the time for action had now arrived. Referr- ing to the cendition of agriculture he said the agricul- tural labourer was living under most miserable conditions .and a large number of them were fleeing to industrial centres where the 18 shillings or £1 looked a princely sum to the 10 or 12 shillings they had been receiving. The land question was a workingman's question. It was at the root of the evil conditions which prevailed. The landed classes had. had complete control of the legislative machinery and they had used it for their own benefit. They (the speakers) had come there that night to advoc- ate that workinemen should now legislate for themselves. With the two political parties they had nothing to do. In place of individualism they were prepared to advance the spirit of communism. They were not pre- pared to believe that the highest ideal had been reached in the scramble for existence in the present day. He did not blame the capitalists and landlords but he did blame the democracy for allowing the present state of things. He appealed to his audience to take action and use the power they possessed for the benefit of the working classes. (Applause.) Mr. LEES said he would like them to think over what had been said that night, and resolve that they would become members of the Independent Labour Party. Their principle was "Truth, righteousness, justice." Mr. J. SWINDLEIIIIRST said they had a social pro- gramme. They wished every man and woman to join their trades unions. If they were true to those interests they would attend their meetings as trades unionists and take part in the election of their officers and have a taste of what conducting business was. Liberal and Conservative capitalists were not Liberals and Conserva- tives when they met in. the Chamber of Commerce. When there was a strike they forgot their Liberalism or Conservatism, and why should they, the working classes, be divided into red or blue. On their backs Liberals and Tories had been carried to Parliament, and had legislated chiefly for the benefit of themselves. He hoped the labour party would have candidates for the Board of Guardians and Town Council, and that they would run a labour candidate against the Tory that represented them in Parliament. Let the working classes have men from their own ranks to represent them, men who knew their needs and would strive to secure them. (Ap- plause.)

The audience was invited to ask questions. Mr. Wm. Diggle wanted to know how workingmen could attend committee meetings in connection with the Board of Guardians and the Town Council, as they were held during the daytime. Mr. Fox replied that it was their intention to have the meetings held in the evening. Mr. Diggle contended that the condition of the working classes was better than sixty years ago. He could remembr the time when people had to live on "skilly." Mr. Battersbv said Mr. Diggle had been decrying work- ingmen's abilities. He was referring to some scandals Mr. Diggle had circulated during the recent Municipal Election when he was called to order by the chairman. Mr. Lees said that Mr. Fox in his speech drew a picture of the condition of the people 150 years ago and not 60 years ago as Mr. Diggle was referring to. A short dis- cussion afterwards ensued in regard to the question of the amalgamation of various socialistic societies as sug- aested by Mr. R. Blatchford. The meeting afterwards disperscd. A ccillection was taken in aid of the funds of the local branch of the Independent Labour Party.

ELEMENTARY EDUCATION IN WARRINGTON.

GOVERNMENT GRANTS.

The report of the Committee of C3nricil of Educa- tion for the year 1893, which has just been issued, shows that during the last two years there has been in England and Wales an increase of 328,859 in the number of children on the registers. The total number of schools existing last year was 19,682, of which 11,928 were National or Church of England, 522 Wesleyans, 970 Roman Catholic 1,290 British, Undenominational, &C., and 4,972 Board Schools. The accomodation provided was sufficient for 5,789,501 children, the number on the registers was 5,153,512, and the average attendance 4.120,457. The average cost per scholar in England and Wales was £2 is. nd. In the county of Lancashire the number of schools was 1,778, the number of scholars on , the rvisters 708,385, and the average cost per scholar in average attendance £1 193. 2d. Below will be found a list of the schools in the Warrington (Borough) School District, together with the number of children in average attendance and the amount of the annual grant for each school. When there are twa numbers given in the , average attendance, the latter denotes evening scholars. ' The letters following the names of the schools have the following signification :—N. National Society, Ch. Church of England, B. British and Foreign School Society, W. Wesleyan Methodist Conference, R. Roman Catholic Poor School Committee, and Fs, Parochial School. ASSESSMENT OF LONDON AND NORTH WESTERN

RAILWAY Co. Mr. Howard, the rating agent of the London and North Western Railway Co., came down to meet Mr. Sutton to consider various matters in connection with the assessments. After some discussion it-wa4 decided that an assessment of gross £5O, rateable £4l 104., should be fixed on such portion of the old high level main line as reaches from the junc- tion of the new main line to the boundary of the rural area, which forms the connect- ing link between the high and low levels. The overseers have raised the question of the rating of the meadows which have become the propevty of the company by arbitration. The railway company through Mr. Howard states that the whole of the land is to let and that. already eight acres have been let to Mr. J. Wainwright, 37, Buttermarket-street, Warrington. Under these circumstances Mr. Sutton and I are of opinion that the rents of the land paid by the tenants will be the criterion of value for as,essment purposes. In the township of Latch- ford I valued for assessment the new station at gross £2lO 10s., rateirble £l7B the old station having been assessed at gross £BO, rateable £6O. The modifications which Mr. Sutton and I proposed for your consideration would make the valuations read as follows :—Buildings, £2.191 at 6 per cent.; £l3l 10s. less 33 per cent. £B7 10s. ; land 9,523 yards at. lid. £49 10s. less 5 per cent. £47; gross, £lBl ; rateable, £134 103. A reduction of £43 103. on the proposed rateable but an addition oil the old assessment of £74 104.

ASSESSMENT OF ENGINEERING WORKS.

_ Messrs. James Barker and Sons engineering works, Haydock-street. This was assessed at gross £2OO 103. rateable £174 103 when the whole of the works were in occupation. Some time back this Committee reduced the assessment to gross £6O, rateable £4B inasmuch as certain buildings in the works only are in occupation. The overseers re-entered the full assessment that inquiry might again be made. I have inspectad the works and find that only so much of the buildings are occu- pied as was the case when the Committee reduced the assessment to gross £6O rateable £4B, and I am of opinion this amount should again be adopted.— All the recommendations were adopted."

HAYD 0 CK.

Pic-Inc.—The choir of St. James' Church. Haydock, through the kindness of the vicar, Rev. E.C. Bowden and the churchwardens, Messrs. E. Jackson, and A.L. Evans, had their annual pic-nic on Saturday afternoon. The party numbering 50 left Haydock, in waggonettes at 1-30, supplied by Mr. Dickson, of St. Helens. Calling at Winwick church, they inspected the interior of the ancient building, and afterwards drove on to Croft, making their headquarters at the General Elliot hotel, where they sat down to an excellent tea. They after- wards enjoyed themselves in various games, and in dancing. Mr. A. Bradshaw proposed a vote of thanks to the vicar and wardens for the trip, which was seconded by Mr. Leyland. The vicar and Mr. Jackson suitably responded, and the party started for home at 8 o'clock, where they arrived at about 915.

- - WARRINGTON SHIRE HORSE SHOW. Sir,—As one of the public interested in this show I venture to ask your insertion of a few words with refer- ence to the awards. In your report upon the foal class you say the position assigned to the colt foal shown by Mr. S. B. Chadwick occasioned some surprise. With others I fully expected Mr. Chadwick's foal would secure the first place and very much surprise was expressed that it was only awarded the third. As, however, they had to meet again at Birkenhead on the following Wednesday considerable interest was evinced in the judging there. It did occasion some surprise at Warring- ton to find the filly in the first place, but there was none at Birkenhead vhcn the 031 t was put in the first place. One is puzzled to know how to account for such a differ- ence of opinion. No change could have taken place in the animals but here at WarriFigton where both parties may be said to be at home Mr. Chadwick's foal is put in the third place, and at Birkenhead removed from the residence of both, the positions are without hesitation reversed. Can any unbiassed person explain? Mr. Chadwick's fraal has only been shown seven times in all with the following results :—A first at Bolton beating Lord Ellesmere's Royal winner, a first at Whalley taking special foal prize, a first at Stalmine and Fylde District, a first at Lancaster, a first at Worstey, a tbird at War- rington, and a first at Birkenhead beating the Warring- ton champion as above stated.—Yours, OBSERVER.

THE RECENT LICENSING SESSIONS. Sir,—We ask permission to say in your columns, that we desire it to be distinctly understood that we are all in full sympathy with the action taken by the Rev.D.W. Roberts, both prior to,and at,the last Brewster Sessions; and, most emphatically, we do not consider it fair that he alone should be singled out for criticism and censure, from which the columns of " The Examiner" have been

free. And we further desire to state that so long as the Watch Committee and the police fail to effectively carry out the licensing laws in this town, some such action as that which was taken at the recent Brewster Sessions is, in our opinion, not only justifiable. but necessary, in the interests, especially, of the young people who are being morally degraded in large numbers by the drunkenness, gambling, and impurity which are known to exist, almost uochecked, in our midst.—We are, sir, yours truly, Signed, - Wu. RECOGNITION MEETING.

- - On Thursday evening a meeting was held in the Baptist Church, Earlestoven, for the purpose of publicly welcoming the Rev. F. E; Miller, late of Redruth, Cornwall, as pastor. The proceedings commenced with a tea to which about 200 persons were present, the tables being presided over by , several members of the congregation. After tea' the attendance was considerably augmented, and the meeting was presided over by Mr. C.E.Mounsey, of Liverpool, who was supported by the Rev, C. F. Aked (Liverpool), Rev. Carey Bonner (Secretary of the Lancashire and Cheshire Baptist Union). The Chairman after alluding to the establishment of the Baptist Church in Earlestown said he had no doubt they had their difficulties,but perhaps they had got over them. These difficulties sometimes arose with the weaker brethren, but very often with the strongere brethren. He was glad to see such a large assembly that night to welcome their friend who now took the pastorate of that place. Mr. Miller came with very good record from the South in Cornwall, and had done good work there in connection with the church, and also in connection with the Association work, and he bad very great oonficience that they would find in him a true man of God. (Applause.) He would build them up and be blessed in the conversion to God of sinners. Nothing would encourage him more than by finding everybody doing soreething to assist the place, and they could help by a regular and practical attendance at the services in the church. (Hear, hear.) There was a good work to be done in connection with the church in Earlestown, -and he thought their friend would find a loving people, and although he had a god record down in the South, he would have as good here, and he hoped this would be the beginning of a good time in connection with the church in Earlestown. (Applause.) Mr. G. F. Clarke, secretary, said the time of Mr. Cottam's departure from the church, and the period of Mr. Miller's advent here had not been a very long one, when they considered the time which elapsed between a pastor leaving, and another succeeding him, in a great many churches in this country. These were circum- stances in connectioa with the church, which rendered it necessary for a pastor to be appointed without delay. Mr. Miller's name was mentioned to them by several, amongst whom was Dr. Cliff red. He was requested to come down, and preach to them, which he did five or six times, and the members of the congregation were well acquainted with the result of those services, and he made a great impression upon the minds of the people. He received a decided call to become the pastor, and the result was seen in his appeerance on that platform that night. They could only hope that the future of their church, under his leadership, would be much brighter than it had been in the past. (Applause.) The Rev. Carey Bonner then delivered the charge to the min;ster. The Rev. F. E. Miller thanked them ter the heartiness of hi- recep ion. From the te ginning, to the present mement,there had been nothing that he could have de- sired. They had made him feel at home, and had en- deavoured to meet whatever wishes he had expressed, and to make arrangements for his own personal comfort and from the whole way in which he had been received there rested upon him a great respnnsibility that he might be worthy of their great kindness. It was his earnest wish to be a friend and helper t oeveryone of them, and he hoped it might be possible to devise means to come into closer acquaintsnce with them, than it was sometimes possible. .He hoped it might be possible to do something fer their children, and for the young men and young women of the congregation. He asked far their trust and prayers ; their love he hoped to gain bye and bye. He might do things of which they did not approve, and ray things with which they did not agree. They might tlenfe he had gone grievously astray from the light path blithe asked them to believe, with all his imperfections, that he came there as one who was striving to be a man of God, trying to do that which would be best for the work of God in that place. (Hear, \ hear). The Rev. C. F. Aked (Liverpool) said that was one of - the really happV days of tiis life. Nothing gave him \ more exquisite plea,sere thae being there that night. With all his heart and soil he wished them well, and he • prophesied that the future of their chureh would have better aud brighter days than ever the past had had ,and that the work of the Lord would prosper in their hands. Be had known Mr. Miller longer than he had known any men there that night. He knew the largeness of his heart, and the greatness of his capacity for service, and they had heard that night his capacities for public work, as they listened to his inspired and inspirinc, speech. Be was glad on their behalf that they had secured as their minister a man of the stamp of Mr. Miller. He was glad that he had come to that church ; he was quite certain that he was going to have a happy time which would be happy, useful, and prosperous. He honoured them for their industry, their perseverance.and integrity, which he himself had witnessed, and for all they were planning to accomplish. If they were going to have a really good pastor they would have to con- tribute to it, and he urged them to assist him in every way possible. Other speakers followed, and the meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the chairman.
