NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Matches "on tour "are, as a rule, of no real value in trying or calculating the form of a team. As a rule they are the reverse of their true form. Frequently some of our strongest teams have gone down on these occasions to a feartul extent. Warrington have been "on tour " during the end of last week, and although they have not suffered defeat, they have been desperately near it. One goal two tries, to one goal one try is not a very sensational victory, but still it is better than writing the letters "L"or " D " against the fixture on the fixture card.

I don't know what kind of sailors our local champions are, but the sea voyage, whatever god it may have none them in health, it had evidently made them very "shaky." They went for pleasure in order to get ready for the severe league matches which are in store. I hope that they enjoyed their outing and that it will act as a good tonic for some time to come. Some of the team came back on Saturday night, but a number stayed dur- ing the whole of the presekt week, so that they have had the full benefit of their holiday.

The team which did battle for Warrington was not exactly as it was chosen. Fair Turner was not present, and his place as skipper was filled by Jolley. Plumpton also did not set sail for Manxland, and Boscow by that means found a place in the team as full back, Heesom going three quarter. The match its2lf was very un- equal in the two portions of the game. In the first, it appeared as though Warrington were going to have a runaway victory,for they scored one goal two tries, while the Manxmen did not get a solitary try. In the second moiety, Warrington were in the same predicament,while Mona got within a try of Warrington. Warrington have previously easily accounted for the Manxinen, so that on this occasion there was a screw loose somewhere.

To-day, the Warrington team encounter Barrow, and they will then give a fair indication of their true form. A great deal will depend upon the play to-day as to who will represent Warrington in the League matches which commence next week. It should therefore be an incenture to the players to do their very best, and to play a combined and brilliant game. The Barrow team area good team nothwithstand- ing that they have been degraded horn the first division to the second. They were singularly unfortunate in their matches last season, for on many occasions they were only beaten by a try. Warrington will therefore not have a walk over. -

The following is the team selected to meet Barrow to day : full back, Beeson ; three quarter backs, Burton, Potts, Evans, and Plumpton ; half backs, James T. Bate, and Carey; forwards, Fair Turner, Jolley, Thorniley Taylor, Nevins, Dakin, Buckley and Berry. It will be noticed that there is a considerable change among the backs to the matches heretofore. Plumpton has al- ready played against Broughton Rangers, but to-day, he will make his de'clit before the Warrington spectators for the present season. Another change is the appear- ance of Burton. This player is well known among junior clubs as a strong and swift three quarter, and he will be a distinct acquisition to the team. He was noted last year almost at much for dropping goals as for scoring tries. It will also be noticed that Carey is chosen as the partner with " Smack" Bate in the half back divi- sion. This will occasion surprise to a great many. He is not new however to this position, for he was a half hack before his introduction into the Warrington team. Nevits has also not previously played at tt arrington this season.

It will perhaps be of some interest to the Warrington football supporters to know how the members of the league are faring thus far this season. I will therefore give them some idea. Oldham the champions have thus far only had one serious match, and that was with Run- corn whom they defeated at Runcorn, so that they are not doing badly by any means. Hurst appears to be as lively as ever. Swinton also defeated St. Helens Recs. on Saturday although it was only by the goal kick. Warrington's doings are of course well known her:. Wigan have already two victories away from home to boast of, though they be but narrow ones. Tvldesley are but moderate, while it is rather difficult to judge of Salford for they have only met Manchester Rangers yet. This club is very weak, and the Salfordians did not for- get to tub it in. As several junior Warrington teams would account for the Rangers this does not count for much. Broughton Rangers and Rochdale Hornets are also of considerable weight this year, the latter eipeci- ally having been strengthened. Ot the two new mem- bers of the league, Leigh and St. Helens, the latter has by far the best record. Looking through the whole lot of them, Warrnigton seems to stand as good a chance as any of getting to the top.

I mentioned some weeks ago that a movement was on foot to form a league of the A teams of the members of the first division of the Lancashire Rugby League. lam glad to b., aisle to state that the idea has " caught on," ard that as a result, there will be a competition this season. The rules have been drawn up, and now await formal sanction of the county committee. The Warring- ton A team have already shown this season that they are very strong, and they should have a very good chance of carrying off the championship. Of course they have only played two matches, hut one of them was against the strong St. Relens Recreation A team and at St. Helens too, and they have won both matches. The for- mation of the league will cause much more interest in the doings of the local A team, and should add very con- siderably to the prosperity of the club.

The Warrington A team made their first appearance before Warrington spectators on Saturday afternoon. Their opponents were no Barton A team,although it was expected that it would be the Barton first team as advertised. The performance of the Warrington men on Saturday augurs well for another successful season for our champion "A " team. The visitors soon after the commencement of the match were completely outplayed at every point, and the display of the Warrington team will no doubt have taught the Bartonians a lesson or two. To run up a score of two goals and five tries to nil was no mean performance for the Warrington players but the score represents the match, as the visitors, so to speak, were " not in it."

The Warrington Exceisiors are early proving their abi!ity in the scoring line. They met the Davenport first team on Saturday on the latter'a ground, and returned victors by cue goal, four tries to nil. It is quite evident that there are plenty of reserves at the dis- posal of Warrington,and that dispite the prognostications of certain prophet 3, Rugby football has still a flourishing time before it. St. Mary's, the junior cup holders of Warrington, and Pemberton Recs., the ditto of Wigan,tried conclusions at Pemberton on Saturday,but neither could beat the other, for the game resulted in each scoring a try, Burtoty,who plays for Warrington first team to-d3v, doin,r the trick fr:,r St. Mary's. As the draw took place away from home, ths S. Mary's team have an excellent chance of making win of it when they meet again.

CRICKET.

The Warrington team concluded their season's en2ag ements on Saturday by administorin2 a de- feat upon the team which hails fr,,rn Brou2hton. I don't know how it is, but when Warringtan are said to , have their test team, they almost invarianly lose, but when they have a "mixed " team, they take it into • tlieir heads to win. This was the case with the Brrugh- ton match. The visitors hrou2ht their stron2e3t team, while Warrington had a " mixed "team, with the result that the " mixed " team got the better of the " strGng- est "team, by 84 to 51,

The Broughton men, unfortunately for themselves, found the Wirrington telm, and Clarke, the wicket keeper,in unusually good fcrm with the result that only Heap made a respectable score of 18. Three of them were run out, and two were stumped by Chrkp. while Rues: took five wickets for 23 runs, and G. Clarke two for 23. This was as it should be. The Warrington tnam can do some smart things if it likes, but it does not always like. Perhaps it would be better next season to "mix" it a little.

After dismissing the Broughton men for 54, Warring- ton set out on their work of overhauling this nomber,and accomplished it for the loss of six wickets. Callon was top scorer with 31, and it was he who made the winning hit. G. Clarke made 23, and Bates 10. Clark, although he only made a round o,tock a long time to make it,for he contented himself with kuppirtg his end up. and leav- ing the scoring to Callon. It is very satisfactory that the two professionals, Bates and Clark, are re-engaged for the next season. They deserve well of the Warring- ton cricketers, for they have done good work during the present season.

The second teams could not come to any definite eon- elusion on Saturday at Broughton. Adamson, the War- rington naptain, won the toss, and went in first, and when 183 had been scored for the loss of only five wickets he closed the innings. Be carried his bat out for an excellent 57. Broughton then went in, but time arrived before they were all dismissed, seven of them howevf r only scoring 93, so that it was a moral victory for War- rington.

WATER POLO.

The Warrington Polo team ;:ti-Qld be well acquainted with the Liverpool Diamond T Club, for the match on Tuesday was the third time they had met this season. The Diamond T have not much cause for congratulation when looking back upon these matches, for everyone resulted in their defeat. The last match, however, was the closest, for extra time had to be played before a goal could be scored. T. Locker was the successful thrower on the present occasion. Although the result was so close, it does not adequately represent the play. It was bad shooting and not bad play in other respects which told against the Warrington team. They are sadly deficient in shooting power, and would do well to prae; tise this a little more.

CYCLING.

The season's runs are fast closing in. To-day an inter- run with the Widnes C.C. is on the card and a very plea- sant time is anticipated the kindliest feeling of good fellowship being very strong between these two clubs.

The Committee of the Warrington Cycling Club are considering the best means of procuring a track for their racing members and for training purposes generally,and as repressive,measures against racing on the highway are yearly becoming more strinent it is to be hoped that their efforts may be successful.

The inter-ftture between the Warrington and Sale clubs was carried out on Saturday and was a grand success throughout. The clubs joined forces at Mere corner,to the number of 50 and rode together into Knuts- ford where on the famous Heath a game at football was keenly contested each club scoring a try. Alter tea at the" Red Cow "a most enjoyable smoking concert and dance wound up the social programme the members rid- ing home at 10.30.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON. The danger of bathing ir the river was well illus- trated at an inquest upon a boy at Sankey Bridges on Monday. The lad had gone with some others along the river side and could not resist the temp- tation to have a "dip." Although the others refused to join him, he went into the river, and was drowned.- It is very foolish for young boys to go into the river even at low tide, for the current is very treacherous, and they are always in danger of being carried away. It is to be hoped that the melancholy affair at.Sankey Bridges will be a warn- ing to others in future to keep away from the river.

The apportionment of free studentships for the continuation schools,to the various national schools of the town has not satisfied everybody, it appears. Councillor Smethurst for instance, thinks People's College has too large a share of them. As the con- tinuation schools are intended for boys who have passed the higher standards, and are abut to leave schooi, it would be very difficult to find a fairer method of apportioning the studentship to the various schools, than that the committee have adopted, viz., according to the number of scholars in the higher standards.

I was glad to see that Councillor Plumpton called attention at the Warrington Town Council meeting on Tuesday evening, to that open sewer, commonly called Sankey Brook. He called it an abominable nuisance, language, which residents near its banks, will consider not in the least too strong. From information he had received, Councillor Plumpton informed the Council that the Sanitary Works Committee were not free from blame as they turned a quantity of refuse into the brook fromlongford Depot. This was denied by Alderman Francomb and Councillor Shillcock,who stated that the refuse came from St. Helens. Councillor Bennett was in favour of the Corporation taking action against the St. Helens authorities for the prevention of the continuance of the nuisance, but in the end nothing further was done. This is no doubt a ticklish subject to tackle, but at the same time it is to be hoped that something will be done for the stoppage of what must be a most serious danger to the health of a large number of the inhabitants of the town.

Some time ago there was a certain Warrington alderman who stated that when he went on business for the ratepayers of the town he upheld the dig- nity of the position. If the statement made at the ouncil meeting on Tuesday that a deputation to London cost twelve guineas each it must be in- ferred that there are others in the Council who don't forget the dignity of the Corporation when they are away at the expense of the Coreoration. Councillor Longton wanted to know whether it was money well spent, but, after all, this is only a matter of opinion. No doubt the members of de- putations think the money is well spent—in fact, very well spent. But, really this deputation busi- ness is getting rather serious, and I should not he surprised if candidates at the coming November elections were asked for a pledge or two on the point,

As the Gymnasium is one of the most useful and deserving institutions in the town, I am glad to hear that a vigorous effort is about to be maae to wipe out the balance of debt which is still owing. Preparations are being made for holding a sale of work in October,and it is to be hoped that the Com- mittce will meet with all the support they certainly merit. A gift of £25 which has been received from Mr. Henry Thornton will also be useful in the same direction. it tiariy be mentioned that, in. order to provide still more completely for the comfort of members of the aymnasium a new caretaker has just been appointed, and, taking everything into account, it is reasonable to anticipate that the sea- son which opened on Monday evening will be a suc- cessful one. P.S.A. AT THE Y. M,C.A.

A successful meeting was held on Sunday last Mr. Kent (president) in the chair. Mr. T. Malkin was the speaker appointed for the day and he gave an earnest address on the "Evils of Gambling " Numerous instances of the evil effects of betting were given, and some cases were cited where the families were left without food in ordcr that money might he put upon certain horses, Mr. Malkin showed how this habit led on to other vices and warned those present of the many dire results which must accrue. to those indulging in the same. Mr. Barber and Mr. G. Holford also took part in the meeting.

WARRINGTON.

ACCIDENT TO ALDERMAN 'HARRISON AT BLACKPOOL.

Alderman Harrison, who is staying at Blackpool, met with an accident on Sunday morning. Ile was proceeding along the Promenade on a tricycle, pro- pelled by hand,when the machine collapsed, and he fell to the ground. He was considerably shaken, but sustained no serious injury. He appeared again on the Promenade in the evening, apparently none the worse for the accident.

GI, but this has been paid off during the last year, and there is now a balance af .C 3 16s. 9d. in band. CHOIR TRIP.

The Buckley-street choir had their annual trip on Saturday, when a visit to Southport was paid. The party left Warrington at 1.15, and proceeded to their destination by special saloon. Favourable weather prevailed, and all enjoyed the out very much. The return journey was commenced at nine o'clock, home being reached safely in about two hours. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Jennings, choir leader, and Mr. Fairhurst, organist, for having organizel the trip. _

ODDFELLOWSHIP AT WARRINGTON.

The fortnightly meeting of the Loyal Friendship Lodge (1087) was held at the Green Dragon Inn, Bridge-st., on Monday, when there was a fair at- tendance of members. P.P.G-.M. James Bramhall was in the chair, and V.G.E. Smith Wood in the vice-chair. P.P.G.M. James Crosby (district secre- tary) presented it beautifully framed emblem of the order to P.G. Thomas Wright for services rendered to his lodge. He said he hoped it would be an in- ducement to other members to take a more active part in the affairs of their lodge. Bro. Knight res- ponded in suitable terms. • HAYSTACK FIRE. _ _

At nine o'clock on Tuesday morning, information was brought to the Fire Engine Station that a hay- stack was on fire in Arpley meadows. The fire engine "Major," and the fire brigade under the command ot ,iup4rintendent Turner was quickly on the spot,and commenced to play upon the stack, which was a large one belonging to Mr. John Cross, butcher. The flumes had got a good hold before the arrival of the fire engine, and it was a considerable time before the fire was extinguished. The stack was insured in the Liverpool, London, and Globe Insurance Company, but owing to the fire arising from spontaneous combustion, the Insurance does not cover it. The damage was estimated at about £lOO.

BAND OF HOPE PIC-NIC.

On Saturday a picnic in connection with the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Band of Hope, was held, the place selected being Frodsham. The party, which was composed principally of children. left Bank Quay Station, by the 12 15 train, and after arrival at Frodsham, they proceeded to Mersey View on the top of the hill, where they enjoyed themselves to their hearts' content. An excellent tea was partaken of at Mersey View at which 82 sat down. The teachers in charge of the children were Messrs. Brough, Corradine, Foden, Hardy, Gorst, Bromley, Sudlow, and Pritchard. After tea, the picnicers returned to the station, and left for Warrington by the 6.57 train, after a very pleasant afternoon's outing.

A FATAL FALL DOWNSTAIRS.

An inquest was held on Monday by Mr. Brig- house, at the Three Pigeons, Tanners-lane, on the body of Edward Blackledge. who died on Friday, from the results of a fall :lownstaim—Martha Barnes, 21.1tolleston-st., said the deceased was her father, who lived at 6, Firth-place, off Froghall- lane. He was formerly a signalman in the service of the London and North-Western Railway Com- pany, hit for some time had received a pension. He wai 66 years of age.—William Blackledge said he returned home from his work about two o'clock on Friday morning last, and found his father lying at the bottom of the stairs. He was dead. He called in his brother-in-law and a policeman. The deceased, who was only wearing a shirt, had evidently fallen downstairs accidentally. Blood was issuing from his head and ears.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." DEATH OF A LOCAL PUBLICAN.

Mr. T. 0. Speakman, landlord of the Saracen's Head Hotel, Wilderspool, died somewhat suddenly on Monday evening. He had suffered • for a long time from a weak heart. Over-exertion led to a seizure ot apoplexy on Friday morning week which caused his death. He was attended by Drs. White and Willett. Mr. Speakman bad attained the age of 59 years. His loss will be regretted by many throughout Warrington .and the district around, as he was well-knoWn and highly respected. Before coming to Warrington he carried on a large catering business in Liverpool,being the proprietor of several restaurants, fie first appeared in this town as the licensee of the Lion Hotel, and from there he went to the Griffin Hotel, in conjunction with which he carried on a posting business near the Central Sta- tion. For some time afterwards he acted as mana- ger of the Conservative Club, and eventually removed to the Saracen's Head. He was for many years and up to the time of his death, a Freemason. He leaves a widow and three children to mourn his lass. The interment took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday. ANOTHER SUICIDE AT WARRINGTON. An inquest was held on Monday by Ntr.S.Brighouse, at the Three Pigeons, Tanners-lane, on the body of Wm. Jones. 6, Catherine-street, who had committed suicide on Friday by cutting his throat. Elizabeth Jones, daughter, said the deceased was 72year4 of age. She and her 'brother lived with him. Since toe death of his wife, which tdak pla,;e about six months ago,he had been very denrealed in his mind. On Thursday last her father had occasion to see Dr. Adams, as he was suffering from bronchitis. Witness left the deceased alone in the house (the son being away) at ten minutes past five in the afternoon of that day, and she did not return until a quarter to 12 as she was engaged until that hour in a shop. Shortly after six o'clock the next marn- ing she head a "gurgling noise" in her father's , throat. She went into his bedroom and found him sitting on the mattress with his throat cut. He held a razor in his right hand, lie only lived about a quarter of an hour after she went to him. tier father had never done anything to lead her to be- lieve that lie intended to take away his life. He had, however, been very depressed since the death of his wife,aled the doctor had also told him that he was unable to go to work —May Haynes said she had known the, deceased for eleven years. He had been very depressed since the death of his wife. He had been heard talking to himself in the street,and had been acting in a strange manner. The jury, after 20 minutes' consultation in private, decided to return a verdict to the effect that deceased com- mitted suicide while temporarily insane.

WARRINGTON CHESS CLUB.

The annua.l—tii-e-e—tir--.of the IVarrington Chess Club was held in their room at the Y.A.C.A. on Tuesday evening. The Rev. Canon Dodd presided. The secretary reported that of the five matches played last season three were won and two drawn. The club was stronger in every way than it had been since its commencement. The treasurer in his statement mentioned that the clst of the champion- ship trophy (won last season by Father Firth) was, thanks chiefly to the liberality of the vice-presi- dents, now defrayed, and the club was entirely free from debt. The election of the officers for the en- suing season was proceeded with. Canon Dodd was re-elected president,Mr.Lincoln Chandler of Sankey, secretary, and Mr. J. S. Garland, treasurer. Mr. Isherwood was elected a member of the club. It was decided to continue the connection with the Lancashire Chess League Association. The trophy competition which last year was on handicap lines will now be purely a championship one, and as this will reduce the chances of the weaker players to a minimum, a club handicap will also be insti- tuted, for which a prize will be offered. These com- petitions will commence on the opening night of the season, October 2nd. The club has very comfort- able quarters at the Y.M.C.A. where the members meet every Tuesday and Saturday evening during the winter months.

Two loads were sent to Mr. Robert's yard. TRAGIC AFFAIR AT WARRINGTON.

A MANUFACTURER SHOOTS HIMSELF. A sad occurrence took place at Messrs. Nelson and Company's Tannery, Mersey-street, Warrington, on Thursday lastw3ek. Mr. Edward Davies, manag- ing partner of the firm, and brother of Mr. Ro'lert Davies, the well-known solicitor in the Market Place, shot himself through the head with a five- chambered revolver whilst in his office at halfpast two in the afternoon. Dr. Cannell was promptly in attendance, but Mr. Davies's condition was hopeless and he expired at twenty-fie minutes past nine o'clock the same night, without having regained cousciousness. Mr. Davies return from Manchester by the train reaching Warrington Central Station at ten minutes to two,walked straight to the works in Mersey-street, and entered the office. He was alone in the office when he committee the rash act shortly after two o'clock. He had placed the re- volver a little above and behind his right ear, and the bullet passed out at the top of the head towards the back, and entered the wall of the room afterwards rebounding to the ground. The clerks from the adjoining room at once ran to his assist- ance, and raised the alarm. Deceased was fifty six years of age, and was very well known in all the leading circles of Warrington. The deceased was a married man, and resided at Acton Bridge, and leaves a widow and family to morn his loss.

===== 0002601_18940915_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INQUEST.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest on the body of the deceased at the Parade Room of the Bridewell, yesterday afternoon. Alderman Bolton was the foreman of the jury which was composed almost exclusively of well-known tradesmen of the town. The first witness called was Robert Davies, who was visibly affected, and gave his evidence in an almost in- audible tone. He said : I am a solicitor practising in Warrington. Deceased was my bnither. He was the managing partner for Messrs. Nelson and Co. tanners, Warrington. He had lived at Woodville. Acton Grange, and was about to remove to Southport. He was 56 years of age. I last saw him alive a few weeks ago, but I have been away for my holidays in Llandudno. He called at my office on Tuesday night wanting to see me, but 1 was out at the time. I saw him on Thursday at 2.30 after the occurrence had taken place and I remained with him until his death. My brother met with a seri- ous accident about eighteen months ago. There had also been great depression in business lately. He com- plained ot loss of memory and had fears for his reason and eyesight and had also fears as the future state. He seemed to recover at times and be well again. When he cams to see me, he told Mr. Thomas how depressed he was.

Alfred Thomas, J.P. of Frodsham. said: Deceased was my brother-in-law. I saw him on Monday in the after- noon about four o'clock. That was the day on which he was loaving Acton Bridge and going to Southport. I found him very much depressed, and he talked about his health, and complained of his eyesight. He would utter a few sentences and then stop and commence crying. He said he was going to Liverpool, and asked me to go with him. We got to Liverpool at a quarter past six. On the way he acted in a similar manner. When in Liverpool he got a ltttle better. When we met his wife we got him into a cab and went back to the station. He was in a de- presPed state, more so than I had ever seen him before. On Thursday morning I came over and told him that his brother, Mr. 11-,bert Davies, was coming from Llandudno, and he told me that he wished to see him particularly. I thought at the time he looked better. He did not cry to the same extent that he did on Monday,although he was still depressed. When I was leaving him he said that he was going to Manchester. I tried to persuade him to be quiet and not to go. He would go, however, and I thought no more about it until after two o'clock when the occurrence had taken place. When I saw him last he complained of a constant " pushing " feeling in his head, and Qaid that he would never get over the acci- dent. He had not behaved strangely before the accident took place, and his manner was clearly attributable to it. I believe that the doctor recommended him not to take stimulants on account of his head, and he had not taken any for about two months. Cross-examined by Alderman Bolton : I believe that the change from Acton Bridge to Southport worried him, as he was fond of a country life. There were no finan- cial embarassments to cause him to make away with himself.

Thomas Birtles, photographer, said : I knew the deceased very well. I saw him yesterday (Thursday),, at the Central Station, Manchester, at about 1.20 p.m. The train was rather late in,and I was one of thefirst off the train. I saw Mr. Davies watching the passengers as they went through the gate. His anxious look attracted my :attention and I stopped to watch him. I think he was looking out for someone, and after the passengers had left the train, he dropped his head as if he was dis- appointed. I remarked to a Warrington friend, " Mr. Davies is disappointed about something. He is waiting for someone and they have not come." He turned hur- re.dly round and walked off the platform. That was ' 1.25 p.m. He walked in the directicn of Albert Square, hut must have immediately turned back to catch the 1.30 train for Warrington. It was his appearance that attract-r 1 my attention. He did not appear as he ought to have done. I moved to him, but he did not recognise me, aV.hough he knew me very well.

- • John Isherwood said : I am a clerk for Messrs. Nelson and Co. I have known deceased 21 years next Decem- ber. lie first commenced being strange after a trap accident in Novemher, 1892. I did not notice anythinu before. Ou Thnrdtv morning he came to the office and onened the letters and read them. He had not boo'rd the work for two or three days, and I had to twr,lc ity own work. After he had finished the letters he a.,ked me to come with him to look at some leather. I went downstairs with him, when he turned back, and I went mviel When I returned, I heard of him going to Man- chester by the 11.55 train. Aft:Pr he went to Manchester I did n t see him again until 2.2 p.m. I then met him and went upstairs to the private office with him. I mentione,l something about the woik and he said that it was all right. He asked me if his brother Robert and Mr. Thomas had come, and I.told him that they were not coming until 3.20. He then went into his private office and pulled the door to without fastening it. I went to a lavatory. Just as I was entering the lava- tory,' was startled by a report. I did not like going into the room mvself.and I rushed downstairs and alred two men to go in. Mr. Letclie, who lives next do arr to the works, also came rushing upstairs, and I believe he was the first to enter the room. I went in a couple of minutes afterwards and found deceased lying on his hack, with his head in the private office, and his fect in thelavatorv. He had a wound in his head, and blood was flowing from it. I sent for Dr. Cannell and the police. and I found the revolver lying between the de- ceased's feet. Although the deceased was low and de- pressed, I have never had any reason to believe that he would take away his life.

By Alderman Bolton : I have never seen him with the revolver before.

Peter Watson, traveller for Skelton Bros., said: The deceased came into the shop on Thursday, a little before two o'clock. He asked me to lend him a revolver as he wanted to shoot a dog. He said that he had one which would not work. While I was getting the revolver and ammunition an assistant took up the revolVer which deceased had had in nis pocket, and found that it was all right. I explained to deceased how it was worked, and he said that he had some ammunition and then went out of the shop. I had never seen the revolver before Thursday. Mr. Robert Davies observed that the revolver was not a new one, and yet he had never seen deceased with it before. Mr. Watson said that the revolver was slightly cor- roded and was fairly new. It was called the" British Bulldog." . _

Mr. Davies said that deceased had a revolver tut it was a much larger one and had come from Australia Dr. Joseph Henry Smith, Acton Bridge said I Faie known the deceased for several years as he resided in sty district. In November 1892 he was out with a sho bki; party and it was rather late. Deceased was comirg to- wards me in a trap when a wheel sank into a hole, and

deceased was pitched out on his head and was totally 1 unconscious for some time. When he had sufficiently recovered he was carrel home, when I attended him for several months. For some time not a single day passed witnout me visiting him. For a• long time he was almost absolutely unconscious and he was incapable of exerting himself mentally in any way. Gradually he recovered from this, and I have attended him irregularly ever since. It was only last Saturday that I had a con- versation with him about his health. He frequently ex- pressed the opinion that he would never get well again. He complained of shortness of memory, and that he could not remember things which occurred the same day. He was not able to add up small columns in arithmetic. I noticed that deceased was very much troubled that he could not , make any calculation at all. He was sleepless, and suffered from loss of appetite, which were symptoms of disturbed nervous centre. I have no doubt it originated from the accident. By Mr. Dalton : The bullet went through the brain, and death was due to concussion. Inspector Cox said that from information he received he went to Nelson's Tannery and found the deceased suffering from bullet wounds in his head. The bullet was lying beside him. It had struck the ceiling and knocked a piece of plaster out and then rebounded to the side of the deceased again. Two letters were found in deceased's pocket. The jury after a short consultation returned a verdict of "Suicide during temporary insanitary," and ex- pressed their condolence with the family at the un- happy event. The Coroner said that he would join with the jury in their expression of sorrow. He hoped that it would be some small solace to the family to see that there was so much sorrow expressed at the sad occurrence.

THE WARRINGTON HORTICULTURAL

SOCIETY.

MEETING OF THE COMMITTEE.

A meeting of the committee of the above society was held in the Gymnasium on Thursday last week the chair being occupied by Alderman Bolton. Mr. RYAN, the hon. secretary, in giving his report said that he had no balance sheet to submit to them at present, but he w,uld give them a rough statement of the finances. The society had now been in existence for seven months, and considering that it was only in its infancy, they could say that it had been successfully carried out. They would be able to carry forward a balance of at least £25. (Applause.) There had been several points of interest in connection with the society, one of them being the greenhouses. The Rev. H.V. Pigot and Mr. Fairhurst had been surprised to find that there was so much interest displayed by the workingmen • in regard to greenhouses in back yards. They c.aisidered that it would be well another year to split up the green- houses competition into two cl..sses. They were work- ingmen, and men who were possessed of good incomes. The latter persons could put up better greenhouses than the former, with everything better adapted for the growing of plants. It was therefore suggested that the Society should offer two prizes, one for the middle classes, and the other for the workingmen, arranging the competitors according to the rental of the houses. With regard to backyard gardens, they found that there were people who had beautiful gardens in their back- yards, and it was rather hard to pass these over. They must have spent a vast amount of labour in getting them to such perfection. The Rev. H. V. Pigot had written to him thanking him for the very satisfactory arrangements made to carry out the judging of the green houses, and suggesting that prizes be awarded for back yard gardens. When they met again for the making the next schedules,he(the secretary),hoped that they would not forget that point. Another matter he might mention was for years past, the corporation had been in the habit of distributing the plants grown in the park to whoever applied for them. The Corporation had now very generously offered to allow the Society to distribute the plants by ticket, and he as secretary, had promised that the Society would undertake to do so. A register of the persons who applied for the plants would be kept, and he thought that the Society could very easily award a few prizes for the best grown of those plants next year. The competition would be confined to cottagers to whom it would be a great encouragement. Another matter which he wished to bring before them was that there was a difficulty in obtaining the use of the Gymnasium any ti me, ail d therefore he would ask them to give their consent to allow him to accept an offer of an office in the centre of the town, of which the charge would only be nominal. The office was at the corner of Scotland-road.

The Committee decided to accept the offer after a little discussion; and it was also resolved to give prizes for back yard gardens. Mr. J. FULTON &vim observed that the society had so far proved a success, and it had asserted itself as a town institution. They had been heartily supported by the corporate officials, and that being so, he thought that they were now in a position to extend their influence as an educational body in the town. He had no doubt that a great many people who visited the show were sur- prised to see that such beautiful plants could so success- fully be grown near Warrington, and were pleased at such a happy result. There was a lot to learn about flowers, and if they could institute a series of meetings in such a way that they would not only be instructive but entertaining as well, that would be the way to for- ward the interests of the society by keeping up the enthu- siasm which had already been created, and it would be , a boon to many. There were very few attractions in the town, and if, during the winter months they could organize meetings of the kind he had suggested, it would only be carrying out the object of the society. His idea was that with the assistance of the secretary they might arrange for a conversazione, and obtain the services of one or two leading gentlemen who were authorities on flowers and give addresses. The admis- sion to the conversazione might be free to members while for others prices such as would suit the public in general. Mr. RYAN suggested that they should combine a chrysanthemum show with the conversazione. They would not be in a position to give prizes but they might reward certificates of merit.

Several members expressed the opinion that sun a show would equal if not surpass the show recently held.

After further discussion, it was resolved that a con- versazione and show be held in November, and a sugges- tion of the ctlairman that a few prins be off red to cot- tagers, was agreed to.

. . Councillor SMETHURST said that the show recently held being the first, it had not been got up without a great deal of trouble and time being spent upon it. He there- fore thought it would be unkind if they did not thank those people who had taken a direct interest in the show and had worked It to such perfection. They must not think him invidious if he mentioned one or two names. He thought th tt the best thanks should be given to Mr. Ryan, Mr. J. Fulton Smith and Mr. Parker and to those who had worked so hard on the days the show was held. They deserved the warmest thanks for the energy they hod put in the matter. Thanks were als) due the judges of greenhouses, and to the Chairin in (Aller,mara Bolton), for his kindness in bringing his orchi..l3 to the show. There were a great number of people attracted by them.

The CHAI RAI AN also sp9k3 in high commendation of Mr. Ryan, th,-! secretary. The vote of th mks Was seconded and cirried unani- mously and the naeetinz then terminated.

THE ROMAN CATHOLIC PORTION OF HINTON CEMETERY.

The CLERK said that Father McMara had not agreed with the proposal of the Authority to place railings between the Nonconformist and Roman Catholic se-tions of Rixton Cemetery but wished either to have a footpath placei between, or to have a portion of the unallotted ground.—The CHAIRMAN said that he had seen Father McMara with reference to the question, and he thought that it would be best to allow the Nonconformists to have the whole of the plot, and to give a portion of the unallotted ground to the Roman Catholics. The plots had been apportioned according to the population of the various sects. This was, however, not a very good method as the proportion was constantly chang- inr;. He thought that taking all things into considera- tion the plan he had suggested would be the best.—The Chairm...n's suggestion was adopted on the motion of Mr. Heaton, seconded by Mr. Jolley.
